6 Long-Term Memory

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why does an agent that can search everything it has ever stored still act on the wrong facts?

A context window holds the working state of one model call, and retained attention state only accelerates computation over that same prefix. Neither decides what an agent should still know across sessions, system restarts, or extended operational horizons. Providing an agent with persistent memory requires an external storage architecture that can index varied experiences, retrieve relevant historical context without polluting active prompts, and resolve conflicting observations over time. However, semantic vector retrieval alone provides no temporal guarantees. Stale facts, superseded code versions, and contradicted preferences score just as highly in cosine similarity as fresh ground truth. The runtime must therefore treat every stored memory as a fallible claim that carries its provenance, the versions for which it holds, and an explicit path to invalidation. Reliability depends on keeping persistent state behind explicit consistency rules rather than trusting the model to reconcile contradictory historical evidence.

Learning Objectives
  • Classify persistent records by their source, evidential role, and relationship to derived search representations
  • Specify a retrieval contract that bounds access, returned content, resource use, and required freshness
  • Choose among pre-staged evidence, a direct read, and model-directed search from how much of the need the task names
  • Select retrieval methods for an information need, use a bandwidth floor to screen exact vector scans against a latency budget, and estimate multi-hop evidence against a serialized-token budget
  • Design a memory write policy for admission, correction, supersession, expiry, and deletion that keeps derived summaries from perpetuating a withdrawn claim
  • Explain version-aware exclusion and fallback without mistaking removal of stale candidates for complete fresh retrieval
  • Evaluate cross-session memory policies by task outcomes, update behavior, and total resource cost
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

6.1 Memory Beyond One Session

Consider the repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) working on its connection-pool defect across several sessions. When the initial session terminates, the subsequent run must reconstruct the operational state: the candidate source tree at revision r18, the diagnostic failure observed at r17, the passing run of test_waiter_notified_on_return, the integration checks that remain unresolved, and an operational note indicating where to resume execution. None of this state survives inside a transient context window once the process exits. Persisting only the final summary message discards critical supporting evidence. Conversely, retaining raw message transcripts without explicit scope creates confusion. An agent reading an unannotated log can easily mistake an earlier failure under r17 for an error in the new candidate patch.

Durable State, closed by checking each retrieved record against its source.

Standard runtime mechanisms do not bridge this gap. Context engineering selects among information already staged for a single model call, while the inference serving system maintains key-value (KV) attention cache state purely to accelerate that computation. Although a prefix cache can outlive an individual request, it remains a derived acceleration structure for a compatible prompt prefix (principle 5), regardless of the storage medium holding it. Long-term memory addresses a different systems problem, preserving information that must govern decisions across distinct invocations. A durable memory system must explicitly resolve what records to retain, how to index and retrieve them efficiently, and how to update or invalidate them when external sources change. In the formal framing of The H·S·A Exposure Space, these records constitute durable State (\(\StateExposureLevel_3\)), persisting across session boundaries.

Architecting persistent memory for an agent requires solving three interconnected systems problems. First, the data model must establish explicit record roles that keep external sources, tool observations, and agent-authored notes strictly differentiated, ensuring that subsequent sessions recognize what evidence each record supports. Second, the system requires a retrieval contract. Regardless of the candidate generation mechanism, every read must report its query scope, the snapshot versions it admitted, and whether the search terminated cleanly or degraded under resource budgets. Third, the storage engine needs a write lifecycle that admits, updates, supersedes, expires, and deletes records while propagating those mutations to downstream indexes and summaries.

The chapter takes these problems in the order a read depends on them. Record roles come first (section 6.2), because every later indexing, retrieval, and invalidation policy depends on knowing what authority a stored record carries. The retrieval contract (section 6.3) then fixes what any read must specify and report, whatever index sits behind it, and the choice between pre-staged and model-directed retrieval (section 6.4) decides which component formulates the query. Three method sections match index structures to information needs, moving from names and syntax (section 6.5) through learned representations and fused rankings (section 6.6) to chains of related evidence (section 6.7). The write path (section 6.8) and storage invalidation (section 6.9) keep the store and its derived indexes truthful as sources change, and the final section (section 6.10) judges a complete memory policy by the tasks it helps finish across sessions.

6.2 Persistent Records

When a multi-session repair agent stores repository source files, test execution logs, and its own progress notes in a single undifferentiated memory pool, a later session can easily treat an unverified hypothesis or an old r17 test log as current ground truth about the code. Those artifacts play different roles in the repair. The repository source defines what the code looks like at a given commit. An execution log records how a test behaved under specific runtime parameters against that commit. A progress note records only where the previous session planned to resume work. Retrieval strategies, cache validation, and deletion policies all depend on keeping these operational roles distinct.

6.2.1 Record roles

Separating those roles begins with where each artifact originates. Version-controlled repositories, production databases, system configurations, and user profiles exist outside the agent’s execution loop, whereas raw environment observations, tool output traces, progress summaries, and speculative heuristics are produced during execution on top of those external sources, and search indexes are derived from both. Grouping all of them under the single label “agent memory” obscures the boundary between a verifiable source and a derived claim. Table 1 categorizes these records by their architectural role in the evidence chain.

A source of record is the designated authoritative origin for a specific claim. For example, the Git tree serves as the source of record for a file’s contents at a particular commit hash, while an authenticated user message serves as the source of record for that user’s operational constraints. Establishing a source of record defines where any downstream assertion must be validated. It confers evidential provenance rather than correctness. A repository commit may harbor a regression, and an inline code comment may contain unauthorized instructions. Under the principle that authority stays with the source (principle 6), authority describes an evidentiary relationship between a claim and its origin, never an unconstrained permission grant. When an agent’s generated output drifts from its source of record, the output still binds whoever deployed the agent, as one tribunal made explicit in the case that follows. For a memory system, the further question is what happens when such an answer is kept and read again.

War Story 6.1: A support chatbot contradicts its policy page (2024)
Context: In November 2022, following the death of his grandmother, passenger Jake Moffatt consulted the customer-support chatbot on Air Canada’s website regarding bereavement fares (Civil Resolution Tribunal of British Columbia 2024). Air Canada’s formal bereavement policy, published on a separate page of the website, explicitly prohibited retroactive bereavement fare adjustments once travel was completed.

Mechanism: The chatbot erroneously informed Moffatt that he could purchase regular-fare tickets immediately and submit a bereavement refund claim within 90 days of ticket issuance, directly contradicting the airline’s published policy. When Moffatt submitted his claim, Air Canada denied it based on the static policy page. Before the British Columbia Civil Resolution Tribunal, Air Canada argued that the chatbot was an independent legal entity responsible for its own actions.

Impact: The tribunal rejected Air Canada’s argument, finding that the chatbot was an integral component of Air Canada’s website and that the airline remained liable for all information published through it. Finding Air Canada liable for negligent misrepresentation, the tribunal ordered the airline to pay CA$650.88 in damages (representing the difference between the full fare and the bereavement rate), alongside interest and statutory tribunal fees.

Fix: The tribunal dismissed the assertion that customers must independently reconcile conflicting guidance across different website pages, holding that the airline was responsible for all information on its website, whether a static page or a chatbot supplied it.

Systems lesson: A generated response is a derived artifact, yet users and legal frameworks treat it as an authoritative corporate commitment. The engineering remedy is to stage the versioned source of record into the model call and constrain the answer to claims that text supports, which is the job of the retrieval contract developed below. Model fluency provides neither evidentiary validity nor legal protection.

Civil Resolution Tribunal of British Columbia. 2024. Moffatt v. Air Canada, 2024 BCCRT 149. CanLII.

Through the taxonomy of table 1, the published policy page functioned as the source of record, while the chatbot’s generated response was a derived artifact that diverged from ground truth. If a customer-support system logs its previous dialogue turns to inform future responses, those conversational turns become agent-written memory. They document past interactions, but they never constitute organizational policy. A store that kept the chatbot’s wrong answer and retrieved it for the next bereavement question would repeat the error with the apparent weight of precedent, unless the record carried its role as a derived answer and a link to the policy version it claimed to reflect.

Observation logs capture empirical execution history. When an agent tests a connection pool, an execution log showing a failure at revision r17 remains valid diagnostic history even after revision r18 resolves the bug. The subsequent passing run confirms that the specific reproducer succeeded under that exact environment configuration. An execution log provides primary evidence of what the system observed during a run, rather than proving the universal validity of any underlying program assertion. Retaining this historical record across machine restarts or worker failures requires durable persistence guarantees.

↪ Durable, replicated logs that survive worker and machine failures are developed in The Trajectory Log.

Agent-written memory synthesizes raw observations into structured artifacts for future execution steps, taking the form of scratchpad notes, session summaries, or learned heuristics. Condensing raw traces prevents repetitive computation and conserves context window capacity. However, summarization introduces its own failure modes, because compression can strip vital boundary conditions or fabricate causal explanations. For instance, the observation that the targeted reproducer passed at r18 is strictly justified by the execution log. In contrast, the generalized claim that connection-pool timeouts always stem from missed thread notifications asserts a universal rule that a single test execution cannot substantiate. Storing an unsupported generalization in a persistent database creates compounding errors in subsequent planning steps.

This divergence mirrors the foundational distinction from cognitive psychology (Tulving 1972) that agent architectures have adopted. Episodic memory captures discrete, time-stamped events anchored to specific execution contexts. Semantic memory stores generalized knowledge, rules, and abstractions extracted across multiple episodes. Procedural memory complements both by preserving executable workflows, tool scripts, and operational runbooks. In the terms of table 1, observation logs and episode summaries are episodic records, generated lessons and reference facts are semantic records, and reusable repair workflows represent procedural records. In an engineered system, semantic and procedural records are therefore derived artifacts whose epistemic validity depends entirely on the fidelity and scope of the underlying episodic records.

Tulving, Endel. 1972. “Episodic and Semantic Memory.” In Organization of Memory, edited by Endel Tulving and Wayne Donaldson. Academic Press.

A retrieval index serves as an auxiliary acceleration structure for locating information across storage tiers. Whether an index stores term postings, learned vector embeddings, or graph edges over source files, logs, and agent notes (structures this chapter develops in turn), an index only locates candidate records. When a search query matches an index entry, a high similarity score demonstrates lexical or semantic affinity to the query string, but provides no assurance of operational applicability to the current task. Operational applicability depends on whether the referenced record matches the current system state, schema revision, and environment constraints.

6.2.2 Read and write lifecycle

Consider the state of a code-repair agent immediately after it writes revision r18. If its vector or keyword index was generated from revision r17, a retrieval query for src/pool.py will return the unpatched file. While that record remains an accurate historical reflection of r17, serving it as current context leads the model to believe the bug persists, prompting redundant or conflicting edits. The staleness that context invalidation guards against thus extends beyond the active prompt into the persistent storage tiers that feed subsequent inference calls.

↩︎ Context invalidation, which withdraws a staged observation’s authority over current state once its source changes, is established in Context Invalidation.

Precomputing search indexes eliminates the need to scan large repositories or execution histories during inference. However, this optimization incurs an ongoing synchronization cost. Every update to a source file requires indexing new content and invalidating or evicting obsolete embeddings. Executing these updates synchronously blocks read or write operations, while background asynchronous indexing opens a lag window in which the index still answers from r17 although the file is already at r18. A query that arrives inside that window needs an explicit rule for what it may return, which section 6.9.2 supplies.

Conversational agents meet the same consistency problem in user state. A user who said “Schedule no meetings before 10:00 AM” later says “before 11:00 AM.” The two directives are nearly identical as text, yet their intent is mutually exclusive. A store that treats the second as a duplicate keeps the stale behavior, and one that appends it without retiring the first leaves a contradiction in every later prompt. Telling a complementary fact from a superseding one requires explicit lifecycle metadata, which the write path (section 6.8) supplies and carries to every derived index, alongside validation, time-to-live (TTL) expiration, and cascade deletion.

The read side comes first, because lifecycle metadata protects the agent only if every read reports it. A retrieval interface must therefore return candidate text alongside the provenance, version, and completeness information on which safe execution depends.

6.3 The Retrieval Contract

Diagnosing the connection pool’s stalled waiter requires both the current implementation in src/pool.py and the test telemetry associated with test_waiter_notified_on_return. A search across repository history can easily return both an r17 test failure and an r18 reproducer pass. While both records are genuine, they answer distinct questions: one documents the defect that triggered the investigation, whereas the other evaluates a candidate patch. Before choosing an index structure or a similarity metric, a system must specify what the agent’s next decision demands and what guarantees a returned record actually establishes.

Definition 6.1: Retrieval contract

A retrieval contract specifies the information requested, the permitted scope and resource limits of the read, and the provenance, applicability, and completion information returned with its results.

  1. Significance: It ensures a bounded search result remains interpretable before that result enters the next model invocation. A misread or misattributed result exacts a double penalty in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2): its tokens consume prompt budget and inference cost during the attempt, while the mistaken decision it induces drives down the acceptance probability.
  2. Distinction: Ranking algorithms merely score and order candidate items under a specific relevance heuristic. The retrieval contract establishes authorization boundaries, version constraints, serialization budgets, and explicit completion semantics.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking an empty result or a truncated scan for definitive proof that the requested data does not exist.

This contract applies uniformly whether the agent executes an index search, a relational database query, or a direct filesystem read; it mandates no single storage layout or similarity threshold. It divides the work among three parties (figure 1). The runtime sets the request’s bounds, the retrieval service checks access and searches within them, and the result envelope reports status, provenance, and limitations before the runtime stages any evidence for the next model call.

6.3.1 The bounded request

A raw information need such as “find the notification logic” underspecifies the retrieval operation. It leaves undefined whether the caller requires the active workspace, an earlier Git revision, or a diff between them. It says nothing about which repositories the principal may access. It also ignores how many tokens the caller’s remaining context window can accommodate. As summarized in table 2, framing retrieval as a bounded request separates these operational constraints from the underlying index mechanics.

A version requirement differs from a wall-clock freshness heuristic. An index entry for revision r17 remains invalid as current r18 source code even if an indexing worker refreshed it seconds ago. Wall-clock TTLs merely trigger periodic reindexing, whereas explicit version tokens establish whether a cached representation strictly matches its underlying source snapshot. When the retrieval service cannot fulfill the requested version from its precomputed index, it must either fall back to an authorized direct read of the primary store or return an explicit incomplete status. Section 6.9 develops this trade-off once the index itself can lag its source.

Similarly, result-count, payload-size, and compute limits constrain different dimensions of system resource consumption. Ten complete source files consume far more serialized tokens than ten function docstrings, yet returning a single item might still require scanning millions of candidate records. The payload limit protects the caller’s downstream context window, search-work budgets (such as graph traversal caps or posting-list scan limits) constrain retrieval service compute, and execution deadlines prevent the agent runtime from blocking indefinitely. Client timeouts must also propagate cancellation signals to the underlying storage engines. Uncancelled background queries continue to consume backend resources and inflate total execution cost.

6.3.2 The result envelope

Returning bare text snippets from a search leaves the caller unable to tell whether the matches came from r17 or r18, or whether zero hits meant the symbol was absent from the repository or the query timed out halfway through the index. To keep search outcomes interpretable, a production retrieval service wraps matching records in a result envelope that specifies the repository scope and snapshot revision actually searched, the terminal execution status, and any operational constraints that qualify the results:

  • complete confirms that the declared search algorithm ran to conclusion under its stated semantics. It is a mechanical guarantee about the search performed, not a claim to have found every relevant fact.
  • partial indicates that the operation halted before exhaustive evaluation, explicitly recording the abort reason (such as an expired deadline, an unavailable index partition, or an output payload cap).
  • unavailable signals that the retrieval subsystem failed to return usable data, whether due to network partitions, authorization failures, or service crashes.

When an operation yields partial results or truncates output to respect payload limits, the envelope provides an opaque continuation cursor so subsequent bounded requests can resume paging without re-executing the entire query. Together with the terminal status, that cursor and envelope decide what the runtime may infer when a query returns zero hits, a rule this chapter calls the absence rule. An empty result proves nonexistence if and only if a fully completed exhaustive scan produced it, and even then only within the scanned scope and revision snapshot. A completed literal string search across the repository showing that a symbol does not occur in revision r18 meets this standard. A truncated or partial scan that returns zero matches proves nothing, because the target may reside in an unvisited partition. An approximate index (section 6.6.2) offers no absence guarantee even when it completes, because it deliberately skips most of the candidate space to save work. A result set that lacks the relevant record may reflect that skipped region rather than the record’s absence from the corpus.

At the item level, every returned record carries structured metadata alongside its raw content: a persistent URI or path, byte or line ranges, the exact source commit or snapshot identifier, and a content hash of the returned bytes. When an item represents an empirical observation, such as a test execution log, the record must document the runtime configuration, environment variables, test revision, and terminal outcome. If an item stores a derived lesson or summary synthesized by an agent in an earlier session, its envelope must link to the primary records supporting that inference and flag its status as a derived model output. If the retrieval engine attaches a relevance score, it must name the algorithm that produced it (a lexical ranking function, a vector similarity, or a learned reranker, each developed later in this chapter). Ranking scores exist on their algorithm’s internal scale; systems must not treat them as calibrated probabilities.

In the connection-pool repair task, the staged payload might pair a slice of src/pool.py from revision r18 with an error trace recorded under revision r17, as in listing 1. The fields outside the two content bodies count against the payload budget just as the bodies do. The r17 trace explains why the repair was initiated, but it provides zero evidence regarding the behavior of the r18 candidate. By packaging each record in an explicit envelope, the retrieval subsystem preserves these applicability distinctions across the boundary between persistent storage and the working context. Envelope labels, however, record provenance without certifying veracity. A cryptographic content hash verifies only that the byte sequence matches a specific snapshot. It does not validate the factual correctness of the text. Similarly, categorizing a record as user input or agent-written memory confers no administrative authority on any instructions embedded within that content.

↩︎ The scope rule and the untrusted span are established in Preserving source and scope.

6.3.3 Evidence staging

Piping top-ranked retrieval hits straight from the search backend into the prompt lets uncalibrated similarity scores, stripped version labels, and uncounted metadata wrappers overrun the context budget or slip stale r17 excerpts into an r18 decision. The agent runtime, never the retrieval backend or a raw ranking score, therefore decides which records enter the downstream prompt: it selects evidence for the immediate decision, keeps the source and version tags needed to interpret each item, and accounts for the full serialized token footprint. Staging is where invariant closure (principle 1) reaches the durable read boundary. The bound below the model is mechanical, because a runtime rule rather than the model or the ranker decides what is admitted. The version labels the runtime preserves are the evidence a later acceptance check needs to tell an r17 observation from an r18 one. Even after those checks, retrieved records remain untrusted spans when fetched from a local repository, because serialization that separates them from system instructions cannot stop the model from following text embedded in a record body, the hazard known as prompt injection.

↪ Indirect prompt injection, delivered through retrieved documents and other observations an agent reads, is developed in Prompt injection entry points.

Accurate payload accounting must measure the entire serialized envelope, including wrapper tags, source paths, version identifiers, and status headers, alongside the raw text bodies. Underestimating this structural overhead leads directly to context exhaustion. The token count in notebook 6.1 shows how metadata overhead changes the number of complete records an agent can stage within a fixed prompt allocation.

Napkin Math 6.1: Making room for retrieved evidence
Problem: A hypothetical model call permits 32,768 tokens in total. Reserving 2,048 tokens for generation leaves the remainder for input. If baseline system prompts, tool schemas, and conversation history occupy 24,576 tokens, how many complete retrieval records can the prompt accommodate?

Math: The retrieval allowance is \(32,768 - 2,048 - 24,576 = 6,144\) tokens. Assume a 128-token response envelope and records that each contain 512 body tokens plus 64 metadata tokens, giving 576 tokens per record.

The maximum number of complete records that fit is 10. Staging these records alongside their envelope consumes \(128 + 10 \times 576 = 5,888\) tokens, leaving a remainder of 256 tokens, too few to hold another complete record.

A naive sizing policy that accounts only for raw document bodies would admit 12 records. When serialized with metadata and envelopes, however, those records require 7,040 tokens, overshooting the allocated retrieval budget by 896 tokens.

Systems insight: Metadata and envelope framing consume context capacity just as surely as document bodies. In this configuration, each record dedicates 64 of its 576 tokens to provenance, causing a body-only heuristic to overcommit the prompt by 2 records. The runtime must reserve capacity for output tokens and the global envelope first, then admit \(\lfloor(\text{allowance} - \text{envelope})/(\text{body} + \text{metadata})\rfloor\) records, remembering that every token assigned to system instructions or tool definitions shrinks the retrieval allowance one for one.

Because subword tokenizers (such as Byte-Pair Encoding) merge tokens across string boundaries, tokenizing text fragments in isolation and summing their counts yields an inaccurate total. Runtimes must measure token lengths on the final, fully serialized prompt structure using the target model’s tokenizer. When a critical record exceeds the remaining budget, the runtime must not silently truncate the text. Instead, it should stage a syntactically coherent excerpt with explicit truncation markers, defer the item to a subsequent retrieval pass, or return an opaque handle that the model can resolve on demand.

Every staged token also incurs concrete inference engine overhead beyond occupying space in the context window. Retrieved context must be processed through the model’s compute-intensive prefill phase and then pinned in accelerator memory as key-value (KV) cache tensors for the duration of the generation cycle or prefix cache retention window. Staging more records increases the token volume that the serving engine must process and retain, although the resulting prefill latency depends on the model, prefix reuse, and serving configuration. Every retained token counts against the resident-fit bound of the serving node’s KV pool, so a generous staging policy reduces how many trajectories the node can serve concurrently.

↩︎ The per-sequence footprint of key-value cache state, and how it limits the number of sequences a node can serve concurrently, is established in The Footprint of Retained Attention State.

Checkpoint 6.1: Interpreting a bounded result

Each question presents a retrieval outcome; decide what operational conclusions it licenses.

The retrieval contract establishes the operational bounds, provenance requirements, and payload limits for each read, but it leaves open an architectural question, namely which component formulates the query. A runtime can predict needed evidence and pre-stage it before invoking the model, or it can equip the agent with search tools to probe the store interactively as discoveries unfold.

6.4 Agentic Retrieval

Predicting every file the repair agent will need before invoking the model either stuffs the prompt with irrelevant code or misses a helper dependency that becomes visible only after reading the first stack frame. Forcing the model to search interactively for files the task already named, however, wastes an entire inference turn on a predictable lookup. Two retrieval architectures divide this work differently. In a pre-staged architecture, the runtime predicts which artifacts will be necessary (such as src/pool.py, tests/test_pool.py, and a stack trace from a prior run) and injects them directly into the prompt before model execution begins. In an agentic architecture, the runtime equips the model with bounded text search, file reading, and symbol-navigation tools, allowing the agent to locate notification logic, inspect the test harness, and follow cross-file references as intermediate discoveries dictate. Both architectures operate over the same underlying storage systems and share identical security boundaries. They differ in when the information need is resolved and which component formulates the retrieval queries.

Pre-staging implements standard retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), where the host system selects external documents and packages them alongside the prompt before inference begins (Lewis et al. 2020). By contrast, agentic retrieval delegates query formulation to the model during the execution loop, interleaving environment observations with subsequent reasoning and action steps (Yao et al. 2023). Autonomous systems designed for repository-scale software engineering (Jimenez et al. 2024) rely on this interactive loop, because search and navigation commands shaped for code inspection prove far more adaptable than a single, static set of top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) retrieved passages staged up front (Yang et al. 2024). Rather than mutually exclusive alternatives, these two patterns represent complementary policies operating over the same underlying data stores.

Jimenez, Carlos E., John Yang, Alexander Wettig, Shunyu Yao, Kexin Pei, Ofir Press, and Karthik Narasimhan. 2024. “SWE-Bench: Can Language Models Resolve Real-World GitHub Issues?” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Yang, John, Carlos E Jimenez, Alexander Wettig, Kilian Lieret, Shunyu Yao, Karthik Narasimhan, and Ofir Press. 2024. “SWE-Agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems.

That trade-off sets the operating envelope of each policy. When the required files are known from the task description or project layout, pre-staging them into the initial prompt avoids spending a full model turn on discovery. When a failing assertion in tests/test_pool.py names an internal helper whose definition resides in another module, model-directed search lets that intermediate observation shape the next query instead of guessing the transitive dependency graph up front. Interactive exploration, however, introduces its own failure modes: an agent can chase unpromising leads, issue redundant searches across familiar directories, or stop probing before gathering enough evidence to verify a fix.

Before introducing an auxiliary index, such as an inverted text index or a vector store, an engineer should benchmark against a live-source baseline. Bounded substring searches and direct file reads inspect the target workspace directly on disk, introducing no external index that can lag behind ongoing edits. When a problem description already specifies exact filenames or literal error strings, these live-source primitives often supply all the retrieval capability the agent requires. Direct filesystem access, however, presents distinct systems trade-offs. Live reads observe whichever version of a file exists at the exact moment of execution. If concurrent processes modify the workspace, separate reads executed across successive turns can straddle modifications and observe inconsistent snapshots. Furthermore, raw literal searches locate character matches without semantic awareness, failing to distinguish an active symbol definition from an obsolete comment or an unrelated log message. An auxiliary index justifies its memory footprint, construction time, and synchronization overhead only when it earns them back. It measurably amortizes repetitive scanning costs across long trajectories, resolves vocabulary mismatches through semantic embeddings, or accelerates traversal over structural relationships such as call graphs derived from abstract syntax trees (ASTs). An evaluation must charge the cost of building and refreshing the index directly against its query-time savings. The chapter prices that charge at its end, once lexical, dense, and graph indexes are in hand (notebook 6.5).

When an agent probes its environment, the runtime can optimize query execution through parallelism. A single model turn can emit several independent tool calls that the runtime batches or executes concurrently across threads. Only queries that strictly depend on earlier observations must block for sequential resolution. Table 3 summarizes the core trade-offs between runtime pre-staging and model-directed probing.

Under both policies, the execution runtime retains authority over security boundaries and resource consumption. The model acts strictly as an untrusted query generator. It may propose a narrower search path, supply query strings, or request specific filters, but it can never expand the authorized workspace, assume a different principal identity, or increase its own resource ceilings. The runtime enforces these boundaries at the tool interface. Tool calling schemas define and constrain the supported request parameters, while tool execution results transmit truncation warnings, resource limits, and continuation tokens back to the agent.

↪ Tool interface schemas that bound which request parameters a model may supply are developed in Tool Interface Schemas.

In production systems, these two strategies often merge into a tiered hybrid policy. The runtime pre-stages a compact workspace outline, an architectural summary, or persistent user preferences directly into the initial context window, giving the model immediate structural grounding. The agent then issues targeted tool calls to inspect specific function implementations or test traces only when those details become necessary. Evaluating such a hybrid design requires balancing the upfront token overhead of pre-staging against the inference latency and token expenditure of avoided tool turns.

The choice among a direct read, pre-staging, and directed probing follows from how much of the information need the task itself names. When the task names its targets, such as src/pool.py or a pinned note, a direct read stages exactly those records and spends no ranking or exploratory turn. When the needed evidence is predictable but unnamed, such as the tests that usually accompany a module, pre-staging captures it in one step at the price of some irrelevant tokens. When each needed item can be found only from the one before, as when a failing assertion names a helper whose definition names a lock, no pre-staged set can anticipate the chain, and model-directed probing pays one model round per dependent hop. A hybrid seeds the first hop by pre-staging or direct read and leaves the remaining hops to probing. Whichever component formulates the query, what comes back depends on the matching mechanism behind the read, whether lexical matching, learned similarity, or structural traversal, and each mechanism answers a different kind of information need.

6.5 Lexical and Structural Retrieval

When the repair agent investigates its stalled waiter, it faces distinct information needs: locating tests/test_pool.py, finding references to test_waiter_notified_on_return, and isolating the function enclosing a suspect synchronization statement. A well-designed retrieval contract does not force these varied tasks through a single mechanism. A known file path requires only a direct file read. A specific identifier calls for literal text search or symbol lookup. A broad symptom description requires ranking multiple candidate passages before the agent can decide which source files merit inspection.

6.5.1 Exact lookup and lexical ranking

When the repair agent already knows a path such as tests/test_pool.py, an exact path lookup retrieves that object directly from the authorized repository snapshot, whereas a literal text search for a token like notify or waiter scans for exact character sequences under declared case and boundary rules. Once a literal search across a repository returns dozens of matches spanning definitions, comments, import lines, and archived failure logs, however, neither exact lookup nor unranked substring matching can tell the caller which file matters most or order the candidate set.

To support repeated lexical queries across large corpora without scanning every byte on disk, retrieval systems rely on an inverted index (Zobel and Moffat 2006). The index maps each distinct term to a postings list containing every document where the term appears. Beyond the document identifier, each posting typically stores the term’s in-document frequency and position offsets. How source text maps into postings depends entirely on the index’s analyzer. The analyzer determines token boundaries: whether it preserves full identifier tokens, splits snake_case and camelCase identifiers, normalizes letter case, or strips inflectional affixes through stemming. Consequently, a ranked match on an analyzed identifier behaves differently from an exact match on the raw string. When an agent requires an exact literal hit, it must either query an unanalyzed exact-match field or verify the returned source snippet directly.

1 BM25 (Best Match 25): The name records one weighting in the “Best Match” series of the Okapi retrieval system, built on the simple approximations to a two-Poisson model of term occurrence that Robertson and Walker (1994) derived. The formula survived decades of learned rankers because it needs no training data, only corpus statistics an index already keeps, which is why it remains the baseline a repository agent can deploy on the day its workspace is created.

Robertson, Stephen E., and Steve Walker. 1994. “Some Simple Effective Approximations to the 2-Poisson Model for Probabilistic Weighted Retrieval.” SIGIR ’94, 232–41. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-2099-5_24.

2 Inverse Document Frequency (IDF): Spärck Jones (1972) proposed weighting a term by how few documents contain it, on the argument that a term shared by most documents cannot tell them apart. In a repository that argument favors exactly the tokens an agent searches for, such as a unique test name or error string, and discounts keywords every file shares, which is why a rare identifier ranks sharply under BM25 while a vague symptom description does not.

Spärck Jones, Karen. 1972. “A Statistical Interpretation of Term Specificity and Its Application in Retrieval.” Journal of Documentation 28 (1): 11–21. https://doi.org/10.1108/eb026526.

When a query matches dozens or hundreds of candidates, ranking determines which documents enter the agent’s bounded context window. The BM251 scoring function balances three factors: term rarity across the corpus,2 sub-linear term frequency saturation within each candidate, and document-length normalization (Robertson and Zaragoza 2009):

\[ \operatorname{score}(\LexicalDocument,\LexicalQuery)=\sum_{\QueryWord\in \LexicalQuery}\operatorname{IDF}(\QueryWord) \frac{\QueryWordFrequency(\QueryWord,\LexicalDocument)(\LexicalFrequencySaturation+1)}{\QueryWordFrequency(\QueryWord,\LexicalDocument)+\LexicalFrequencySaturation(1-\LexicalLengthNormalization+\LexicalLengthNormalization\lvert \LexicalDocument\rvert/\MeanDocumentLength)}, \qquad \operatorname{IDF}(\QueryWord)=\ln\left(1+\frac{\IndexedDocumentCount-\QueryWordDocumentFrequency(\QueryWord)+0.5}{\QueryWordDocumentFrequency(\QueryWord)+0.5}\right). \tag{1}\]

Here \(\LexicalQuery\) contains the query terms, \(\LexicalDocument\) is an indexed document, \(\QueryWordFrequency(\QueryWord,\LexicalDocument)\) is the term frequency of \(\QueryWord\) in \(\LexicalDocument\), \(\lvert \LexicalDocument\rvert\) is the document length in analyzer terms, and \(\MeanDocumentLength\) is the collection’s mean document length. The corpus contains \(\IndexedDocumentCount\) documents in total, of which \(\QueryWordDocumentFrequency(\QueryWord)\) contain query term \(\QueryWord\). These lengths are analyzer counts, not the foundation model’s token counts. These symbols are local to lexical ranking. In particular, \(\LexicalDocument\) and \(\LexicalLengthNormalization\) do not carry the meanings earlier chapters gave those letters. The tuning parameter \(\LexicalFrequencySaturation>0\) controls term-frequency saturation. At a fixed document length, the frequency factor rises toward \(\LexicalFrequencySaturation+1\) as \(\QueryWordFrequency(\QueryWord,\LexicalDocument)\) grows, so each additional occurrence adds less to the score than the one before. The parameter \(\LexicalLengthNormalization\in[0,1]\) modulates document-length normalization, with \(\LexicalLengthNormalization=1\) fully penalizing documents proportional to their relative length and \(\LexicalLengthNormalization=0\) disabling length normalization entirely. Because ranking depends on these mechanics, document chunk boundaries, analyzer choices, and parameter settings alter candidate scores even when the underlying source text remains unchanged.

Example 6.1: A higher lexical score is a ranking decision
Scenario: Consider a hypothetical index of 100,000 documents with a mean length of 400 analyzer terms. A single query term appears in 5 documents. With \(\LexicalFrequencySaturation=\) 1.2 and \(\LexicalLengthNormalization=\) 0.75, its inverse document frequency is 9.808.

Analysis: A short candidate contains 80 terms and 1 occurrence of the query term. A long candidate contains 4,000 terms and 6 occurrences. The length term \(\LexicalFrequencySaturation(1-\LexicalLengthNormalization+\LexicalLengthNormalization\lvert \LexicalDocument\rvert/\MeanDocumentLength)\) in the denominator of equation 1 is 0.48 for the short candidate but 9.3 for the long one, so the long candidate’s extra occurrences are divided by a far larger denominator. Substituting these counts into the scoring rule gives 14.58 for the short candidate and 8.46 for the long candidate. Repetition alone does not determine their order: saturation and length normalization outweigh the long candidate’s additional occurrences.

Systems lesson: Rank orders candidates for inspection. If the short candidate is a comment while the long one holds the implementation, the agent still needs source inspection or structural information, and choosing whole files rather than functions as documents would change both scores.

6.5.2 Structural evidence

A lexical match on notify finds the token inside src/pool.py and inside an archived r17 failure log alike, but it cannot return the complete enclosing function or trace which test calls the method that invokes notify(). Structural retrieval recovers that program organization: the enclosing function, type declarations, lexical scopes, and identifiers in call expressions. A parser translates source text into abstract syntax trees with explicit spans, while symbol resolution and semantic analysis establish directed relationships between definitions and call sites. While parsing a call expression identifies the call site syntactically, resolving which concrete implementation executes at runtime requires interprocedural analysis.

How much of that caller-callee chain a structural index recovers depends on language semantics, dependencies, and build configuration. Dynamic dispatch, reflection, generated code, and cross-process calls frequently escape static resolution. In a structural index, a missing edge therefore indicates a gap in static coverage rather than an unreachable path, while a static call edge records a potential invocation rather than an executed one. Figure 2 contrasts the two representations on the connection-pool repair: whereas the lexical index maps notify to flat term frequencies in src/pool.py and r17.log alike, the structural index links test_waiter_notified_on_return to the pool’s return method and onward to _available.notify() while flagging dynamic calls whose targets remain unresolved.

Table 4 separates the questions these methods answer. Lexical indexes can carry path and symbol metadata, but text matching alone establishes no semantic relationship.

6.5.3 Search scope

When a search service selects its top-ranked candidates globally across the repository and only afterward discards results outside src/pool.py and tests/test_pool.py (post-filtering), it can easily return an empty set while eligible matches sit farther down the global ranking. Restricting an initial probe to src/pool.py and tests/test_pool.py focuses the evidence and limits noise only when filtering and ranking are coordinated and the retrieval interface explicitly reports that bounded scope. A subsequent probe can then widen the scope to investigate callers across the repository. Restricting candidates before final scoring (pre-filtering) avoids the post-filtering drop, as does continuing postings traversal until the requested quota of filtered documents is filled. When query budgets or latency limits terminate the search early, the result remains partial.

Choosing the evaluation order is a cost-based optimization decision analogous to access path selection in relational database optimizers (Selinger et al. 1979).3 The decision balances the filter’s selectivity against the rarity of the query terms. A selective filter, such as one that isolates two files in a repository of thousands, favors pre-filtering candidate documents and evaluating BM25 scores only on the survivors. Conversely, a broad filter paired with a rare identifier favors scoring first, because the rare term’s postings list is already short, provided the search engine continues scanning until enough eligible results remain. This trade-off reemerges with even sharper operational hazards when searching vector proximity graphs (section 6.6).

3 Cost-Based Access Path Selection: The System R optimizer chose among index scans, sequential scans, and join orders by estimating each predicate’s selectivity and the pages each plan would touch (Selinger et al. 1979). A retrieval gateway faces the same estimate with a scope filter as the predicate and a postings list or vector index as the access path, and a wrong estimate costs either a scan of the whole corpus or an empty result that looks like absence.

Selinger, Patricia Griffiths, Morton M. Astrahan, Donald D. Chamberlin, Raymond A. Lorie, and Thomas G. Price. 1979. “Access Path Selection in a Relational Database Management System.” Proceedings of the 1979 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 23–34. https://doi.org/10.1145/582095.582099.

6.5.4 Vocabulary mismatch

Early in an investigation, the agent may know only an observed symptom such as “a connection is returned, but another caller keeps waiting,” phrased in words the source code never uses, so exact and structural lookups return nothing. This failure is the vocabulary mismatch problem, where code authors and users describe identical concepts using distinct vocabularies (Furnas et al. 1987). Heuristic techniques like identifier splitting, synonym expansion, or query reformulation can recover some lexical matches. Once an exploratory search identifies a concrete test name, literal lookup is far more direct than further conceptual search. When lexical heuristics still leave the candidate set inadequate, the agent needs a representation that maps descriptive prose to differently worded source implementations, along with hybrid rankers that combine dense semantic similarity with exact lexical precision without mistaking similarity for verification.

6.6 Dense and Hybrid Retrieval

When an agent investigates a bug report describing a caller that hangs indefinitely, lexical search across the repository can easily rank the critical return path in src/pool.py low or miss it entirely. The line that matters executes notify(), but the report describes a caller that keeps waiting. Dense retrieval supplies a complementary candidate-generation mechanism, using learned representations to match a query against records that share underlying meaning rather than literal tokens. Because dense and lexical methods fail in different places, production systems usually run both and combine their rankings (section 6.6.3).

6.6.1 Dense retrieval

Running a neural model over every repository file on each search would make retrieval the slowest step in the agent loop. Dense retrieval avoids per-query corpus scoring by encoding a query and each searchable record as vectors in a shared geometric space and ranking records by vector similarity.4 In a bi-encoder architecture, records are embedded offline before a request arrives, leaving only the query to be encoded online at request time (Karpukhin et al. 2020). This separation removes heavy embedding inference from the critical query path. Offline encoding, however, creates an ongoing maintenance obligation. Newly inserted or modified records require fresh vector generation, and upgrading or fine-tuning the underlying encoder invalidates the entire index, which demands a complete corpus re-embedding.

4 Vector Space Model: Ranking documents by the angle between a query vector and each document vector predates learned encoders; Salton et al. (1975) represented every document as a vector of term weights and scored it by cosine similarity. Dense retrieval keeps the geometry and replaces sparse, interpretable term weights with learned components, which is what lets it bridge vocabulary and also why no single component explains a match.

Salton, G., A. Wong, and C. S. Yang. 1975. “A Vector Space Model for Automatic Indexing.” Communications of the ACM 18 (11): 613–20. https://doi.org/10.1145/361219.361220.

The distance metric is defined by the interaction between the encoder and index configurations. For example, inner product search is mathematically identical to cosine similarity when vectors are unit-normalized (\(\ell_2 = 1.0\)), which shifts the computational cost of vector normalization to indexing time rather than paying for vector division on the query path. Neither metric, however, produces a calibrated probability that a candidate record answers the agent’s question. They represent uncalibrated geometric affinities.

In the software repair task, that geometric affinity bridges the vocabulary gap between the bug report and the return-and-notify code, yielding a candidate for inspection rather than proof of a defect, but the same semantic abstraction blurs fine syntactic distinctions on which execution depends. In src/pool.py, the return path invokes self._available.notify(), the shutdown routine invokes self._available.notify_all(), and acquisition invokes self._available.wait(timeout). All three statements share the same condition variable, module context, and conceptual vocabulary of thread synchronization. Consequently, an encoder often maps all three to neighboring points in vector space when queried about thread unblocking. Their operational semantics are nonetheless entirely distinct: one wakes a single waiter, another wakes every waiter, and the third blocks. An exact lexical search for notify( isolates the call site immediately, and BM25 assigns the rare identifier a high inverse document frequency (IDF). The systems challenge is therefore determining which retrieval mechanism surfaces the precise evidence the workload requires.

6.6.2 Exact and approximate vector search

Scoring a query vector against every vector in an unfiltered multi-million-record store streams gigabytes of embeddings from memory on every search. Building an approximate index, by contrast, is unnecessary complexity when a tenant or file filter already narrows the eligible set to a few thousand vectors. An exact vector scan scores every vector in the eligible search set under the chosen distance metric. It serves as the baseline ground truth for measuring what an approximate search misses, and it remains a practical serving method whenever the eligible candidate set is small enough to stream within the latency budget.

The resource requirements of an exact scan follow directly from dimensional arithmetic, independent of any specific hardware architecture. Let \(\EligibleVectorCount\) be the number of eligible vectors, \(\EmbeddingDimension\) their dimension, and \(\ElementStorageSize\) the storage width in bytes per component. Like the ranking symbols before them, these letters are local to vector search. Traversing the stored vectors requires streaming \(\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize\) bytes from memory, so on a memory system that sustains bandwidth \(\Bandwidth\) one scan takes at least \(\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize/\Bandwidth\). Counting two floating-point operations per multiply-accumulate operation, dot-product scoring requires approximately \(2\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\) FLOPs. These totals account only for the vector inner products. They exclude the latency of query embedding, top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) heap maintenance, and fetching the full record payloads from persistent storage.

↪ The roofline model and the ridge point that separates bandwidth-bound from compute-bound work are developed in Cost Asymmetry Between Input Prefill and Output Generation.

Napkin Math 6.2: Why an exact vector scan is bandwidth-bound
Problem: Consider a hypothetical searchable set of 10,000,000 vectors, each with 768 components stored in FP16 at 2 bytes per component, held in the main memory of a reference server CPU. Which resource limits one exact dot-product scan, and what floor does it set?

Math: Multiply the vector count by the dimension and component width:

\[ \EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize = 10^7 \times 768 \times 2\ \text{bytes}. \]

The raw vectors occupy 15.36 GB. Counting two FLOPs per multiply-add gives \(2\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\), or 15.36 GFLOP per scan. Arithmetic intensity, the FLOPs performed per byte moved from memory, is therefore \(2\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension/(\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize) = 2/\ElementStorageSize\), which is 1 FLOP/byte at FP16 whatever the corpus size or dimension. The reference host delivers 1 TFLOP/s and 50 GB/s, a ridge point of 20 FLOP/byte, the intensity below which memory bandwidth rather than arithmetic limits the rate. The scan sits far below the ridge, so bandwidth sets its floor. Streaming the vectors takes at least 307.2 ms, while the arithmetic alone would take 15.36 ms. A trajectory that issues 20 such searches spends at least 6.1 s moving vectors. Because the floor is linear in the eligible count, a scope filter that leaves 10,000 vectors has a streaming floor of 0.31 ms. A 10 ms search budget sets an optimistic maximum of ~326,000 vectors on this host before accounting for the rest of the request.

Systems insight: The intensity of an exact scan is fixed by the component width, so added arithmetic never speeds it up, and an accelerator’s higher ridge leaves it bandwidth-bound there too. The levers are fewer bytes per vector (compression) and fewer vectors touched (a narrower eligible set or an approximate index). The bandwidth floor screens out exact scans that cannot meet the search budget. When that floor fits, benchmark the complete request, including query encoding, top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) selection, and record fetches. Choose an exact scan when its measured request latency meets the budget. An approximate index must justify its maintenance cost and recall loss against that baseline.

The memory-bandwidth limit behind output-bound latency (principle 2) therefore reappears in retrieval, because an exact scan, like a decode step, performs only a few operations for each byte it streams. The same arithmetic shows where an exact scan remains the right choice. When selective metadata filters restrict the search space to thousands of eligible vectors, an exact scan streams megabytes rather than gigabytes, reducing the streaming floor to sub-millisecond times without the operational overhead of graph maintenance or quantization codebooks.

The exact-scan streaming floor grows with eligible vectors; the search budget sets an optimistic candidate ceiling.

5 Approximate Nearest Neighbors: Exact nearest-neighbor structures that work well in two or three dimensions degrade toward a full scan as dimension grows, the curse of dimensionality. Indyk and Motwani (1998) relaxed the problem to returning a point within a factor of the true nearest distance and showed, through locality-sensitive hashing, that the relaxation admits sublinear query time. Every ANN index an agent uses makes the same bargain, so its misses are part of the contract, measured as recall, never a defect to be tuned away.

Indyk, Piotr, and Rajeev Motwani. 1998. “Approximate Nearest Neighbors: Towards Removing the Curse of Dimensionality.” Proceedings of the Thirtieth Annual ACM Symposium on Theory of Computing (STOC), 604–13. https://doi.org/10.1145/276698.276876.

When candidate sets grow into millions of vectors and queries must return within tight latency budgets, scanning the entire corpus becomes prohibitive. Approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) search5 trades mathematical exactness for orders-of-magnitude reductions in latency and memory traffic, pruning the search space at the cost of occasionally missing true nearest neighbors. Systems cut memory traffic in one of two ways: by traversing a proximity graph to evaluate only a tiny fraction of the vector population, or by partitioning the space and compressing vector representations with quantization codes.

A hierarchical navigable small-world (HNSW) index organizes high-dimensional vectors into a multi-layer proximity graph (Malkov and Yashunin 2018). Layer 0 contains every vector in the dataset. Each inserted vector is also assigned a maximum layer \(\ell = \lfloor -\ln \HnswLevelDraw \cdot \HnswLevelNormalization \rfloor\). Here \(\HnswLevelDraw\) is drawn uniformly from \((0, 1]\), and \(\HnswLevelNormalization = 1 / \ln \HnswMaximumConnectionCount\), where \(\HnswMaximumConnectionCount\) is the maximum number of bidirectional connections per node per layer. The assignment gives each vector probability \(\HnswMaximumConnectionCount^{-\HnswLayerIndex}\) of reaching layer \(\HnswLayerIndex\) or above, so each layer holds roughly \(1/\HnswMaximumConnectionCount\) as many nodes as the one beneath it. This exponentially thinning hierarchy mirrors the express transit lanes of a skip list (Pugh 1990). Traversal begins at an entry point in the sparsest top layer, greedily hopping to whichever neighbor minimizes distance to the query vector \(\SearchQueryEmbedding\). When no neighbor yields further progress, the search drops down to that node’s counterpart in the next layer and resumes the greedy walk. Upon reaching layer 0, the traversal switches from greedy routing to a beam search that maintains a priority queue of the \(\HnswSearchBeamWidth\) closest candidates discovered so far. The search terminates when the closest unexplored neighbor cannot improve the current candidate set, returning the top \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) vectors. The beam width \(\HnswSearchBeamWidth\) is the primary query-time recall knob. A vector the beam never visits cannot be returned (figure 3), so widening the beam evaluates more distances and recovers more true neighbors at the cost of higher query latency.

Pugh, William. 1990. “Skip Lists: A Probabilistic Alternative to Balanced Trees.” Communications of the ACM 33 (6): 668–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/78973.78977.

Constructing an HNSW graph requires executing this layered search for every incoming vector and establishing bidirectional links to its nearest neighbors in each layer. This graph topology incurs substantial memory overhead beyond the raw vectors. Deletions are harder still, because severing a node without reconstructing edge paths among its neighbors risks partitioning the graph and stranding downstream vectors. Production systems therefore mark a deleted node with a tombstone, an exclusion marker that hides it from results while its links stay in place, and section 6.9 examines how the cost of that compromise compounds.

Rather than navigating a graph over full-precision vectors, an inverted file index combined with product quantization (IVF-PQ) reduces memory traffic by partitioning the vector space and lossily compressing the stored representations (Jégou et al. 2011). It has an offline build pipeline and an online query pipeline (figure 4). During index construction, coarse \(\Glyph{k}\)-means clusters the corpus into \(\CoarseCellCount\) Voronoi cells (each cell is the region of vectors nearer its centroid than any other centroid), each backed by an inverted list of vector identifiers. At query time, the system computes the distance from the uncompressed query vector to all \(\CoarseCellCount\) coarse centroids and evaluates only the vectors residing in the \(\ProbedCellCount\) closest cells.

Product quantization then compresses every stored vector. It splits the \(\EmbeddingDimension\) dimensions into \(\PqSubvectorCount\) distinct subvectors of dimension \(\EmbeddingDimension/\PqSubvectorCount\). For each subspace, \(\Glyph{k}\)-means trains a codebook of 256 centroids, allowing any subvector to be represented by a single one-byte index \(\PqCentroidCode_j \in [0, 255]\) indicating its nearest centroid. Storing a compressed vector therefore requires only \(\PqSubvectorCount\) bytes. At query time, the uncompressed query vector \(\SearchQueryEmbedding\) is segmented into the same \(\PqSubvectorCount\) subspaces. Because squared Euclidean distance in \(\EmbeddingDimension\) dimensions decomposes into the sum of squared distances across the \(\PqSubvectorCount\) orthogonal subspaces, the runtime precomputes an asymmetric distance lookup table \(\text{LUT}[\PqSubspaceIndex, \PqCentroidCode]\) storing the squared Euclidean distance between the query’s \(\PqSubspaceIndex\)-th subvector and each of the 256 centroids \(\PqCentroidCode\) in subspace \(\PqSubspaceIndex\). Approximating each stored subvector by its assigned centroid \(\PqCentroidCode_j\) reduces the total distance between the query \(\SearchQueryEmbedding\) and a stored quantized vector \(\StoredIndexVector\) with codes \(\PqCentroidCode_1, \dots, \PqCentroidCode_{\PqSubvectorCount}\) to summing one table lookup per subspace:

\[ \PqApproximateDistance(\SearchQueryEmbedding, \StoredIndexVector) = \sum_{j=1}^{\PqSubvectorCount} \text{LUT}[\PqSubspaceIndex, \PqCentroidCode_j]. \tag{2}\]

Equation 2 transforms a bandwidth-bound \(\EmbeddingDimension\)-component dot product into \(\PqSubvectorCount\) single-byte reads and table lookups. Because the lookup table is small enough to stay in processor cache, each scored vector costs \(\PqSubvectorCount\) bytes of memory traffic instead of \(\EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize\), a reduction by the factor \(\EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize/\PqSubvectorCount\) in exactly the term that bounded the exact scan. The parameter \(\ProbedCellCount\) balances recall against the number of cells scanned, while the subvector count \(\PqSubvectorCount\) trades distance fidelity against bytes per code. Systems commonly recover distance accuracy by re-scoring a short candidate list against full-precision vectors stored on secondary media or remote memory. The same design scales to billions of vectors on accelerators (Johnson et al. 2019). The quantization codebooks and coarse centroids, however, are calibrated on the vector distribution present during index construction. As the corpus drifts or the embedding model changes, quantization error grows until the index is retrained.

Sizing both designs for the 10-million-vector corpus of the exact-scan notebook shows what each one costs in memory. With 32 eight-byte links per node, an HNSW graph adds 2.56 GB of edge topology to 15.36 GB of vectors. Because greedy routing traverses arbitrary graph links, memory access patterns are dominated by dependent random reads, requiring both the graph and the vectors to remain memory-resident. Product quantization with 64 subvectors of 12 dimensions stores 0.64 GB of codes, 24× smaller than the FP16 vectors, and its per-query lookup table occupies only 65.5 KB. Table 5 sets the three designs side by side.

Selecting among these index structures requires evaluating them against the specific record volumes, query distributions, and latency budgets the agent encounters. Two evaluation metrics answer different questions here. Recall against an exact scan measures algorithmic fidelity to the mathematical distance metric, whereas end-to-end task success measures whether the retrieved records contain the requisite causal evidence to resolve the agent’s objective.

The filtering hazard from lexical search (section 6.5.3) returns here in sharper form, because scope filters present greater friction on a graph index than on inverted postings lists. In lexical retrieval, intersecting a postings list with an eligibility bitset prunes unauthorized documents without altering how the remaining documents are traversed. In an HNSW graph, restricting search during traversal (pre-filtering) forces the beam to step exclusively onto eligible nodes. If a tenant boundary or file path mask isolates regions of the graph, the greedy walk stalls at a local minimum when all outgoing links lead to masked nodes. The search terminates prematurely and returns incomplete results, even when highly relevant eligible nodes exist just beyond the masked barrier. Conversely, filtering after an unconstrained search (post-filtering) risks returning an empty set whenever the top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) candidates fall outside the authorized scope. Systems escape the dilemma in one of three ways (figure 5). They partition the index into scope-isolated subgraphs, seed extra entry points inside the authorized region, or fall back to an exact scan when the filter is selective enough that the measured full request meets the budget. The floor in notebook 6.2 serves as an initial feasibility screen for that fallback.

6.6.3 Rank fusion and reranking

Running both lexical search and dense retrieval covers each method’s blind spots, but combining their outputs naively fails in two ways. Adding unbounded BM25 scores directly to cosine similarities in \([-1, 1]\) skews the combined order toward whichever scale is larger. Deduplicating hits by file path alone merges a stale r17 span with its patched r18 counterpart or treats identical code in another tenant as authorized local evidence. Production systems that combine multiple candidate-generation mechanisms through hybrid retrieval must therefore resolve candidate identity by tenant scope, source path, commit or version identifier, and span coordinates before merging lists.

Because lexical scores and dense similarity scores reside on non-comparable scales, directly normalizing or adding raw scores introduces calibration instability. Reciprocal rank fusion (RRF) bypasses score calibration by combining ranked lists based solely on candidate ordinal positions (Cormack et al. 2009). Let \(\FusionInputList_i\) denote the list returned by method \(\RetrievalMethodIndex\), and let \(\FusionInputRank_i(\FusionCandidate)\) represent the 1-based rank of candidate \(\FusionCandidate\) within that list. For a positive smoothing parameter \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant\), the truncated RRF score is defined as:

\[ \operatorname{RRF}(\FusionCandidate) = \sum_{i:\,\FusionCandidate\in \FusionInputList_i} \frac{1}{\RankFusionSmoothingConstant + \FusionInputRank_i(\FusionCandidate)}. \tag{3}\]

A candidate absent from a given list contributes zero from that source. Increasing \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant\) compresses the score differential between adjacent ranks without making the method entirely parameter-free.6 The runtime must also define an explicit tie-breaking convention and configure candidate cutoff depths for each retrieval path.

6 RRF score monotonicity: With fixed \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant > 0\), moving an item from rank \(\FusionInputRank\) to \(\PromotedFusionInputRank < \FusionInputRank\) in one input list increases its contribution by \(1/(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant+\PromotedFusionInputRank)-1/(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant+\FusionInputRank)>0\) (Cormack et al. 2009). If its contributions from other lists do not decrease, its total score increases. This is a property of the item’s score, not a guarantee of improved final rank when other candidates also change, or of relevant evidence.

Cormack, Gordon V, Charles LA Clarke, and Stefan Buettcher. 2009. “Reciprocal Rank Fusion Outperforms Condorcet and Individual Rank Learning Methods.” Proceedings of the 32nd International ACM SIGIR Conference on Research and Development in Information Retrieval, 758–59.

To see how RRF rewards consensus across divergent retrieval mechanisms, consider an illustrative repair query evaluated on the authorized r18 snapshot. Let A be the return-and-notify implementation span, B the reproducer test, and C, D, and E three additional candidate records. Suppose lexical search produces the ranking B, A, D, while dense retrieval produces C, A, E. With the standard choice \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant =\) 60, equation 3 assigns candidate A two second-place contributions, \(1/(60+2) + 1/(60+2)\), whereas candidate B receives only a single first-place contribution of \(1/(60+1)\). Summing every candidate’s contributions in table 6 puts candidate A at the top fused rank on consensus across both retrieval paths, even though neither individual retriever ranked it first.

Dual consensus elevates candidate A for closer inspection, but agreement across two candidate generators remains an incomplete guarantee of relevance when both retrievers share systematic blind spots. To establish higher precision before staging evidence into the agent’s context window, systems employ a cross-encoder reranker (Nogueira and Cho 2019). Rather than scoring query and document independently, a cross-encoder runs the query and the candidate through the model together as one input, so every query token can attend to every candidate token. Because each score depends on a specific query and candidate pair, nothing can be computed before the query arrives, and this joint pass captures fine-grained token interactions at substantial query-time cost. A reranker can only reorder candidates already surfaced by upstream generators. It cannot retrieve missing records, and its score reflects structural and semantic resemblance rather than verified task applicability.

Nogueira, Rodrigo, and Kyunghyun Cho. 2019. “Passage Re-Ranking with BERT.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1901.04085, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1901.04085.

The substantial computational demand of this joint pass is why rerankers operate exclusively on short candidate pools. In a bi-encoder design, candidate vectors are computed offline, reducing query-time evaluation to a lightweight vector dot product. In a cross-encoder, query and candidate tokens must be processed jointly through the transformer layers, preventing offline precomputation. Computational cost scales with both candidate pool depth and token sequence length. For example, reranking a pool of 50 candidates of 300 tokens each against a 100-token query requires processing 20,000 tokens, a prefill load comparable to a substantial model call, spent entirely on ordering. Systems manage this latency budget by constraining candidate pool sizes, invoking rerankers conditionally based on query ambiguity, or executing reranking asynchronously off the interactive path. The complete hybrid pipeline therefore narrows as it proceeds (figure 6). Two candidate generators feed a fusion step that needs only ranks, the costly reranker sees only the short fused pool, and the runtime stages a small labeled set within the payload budget.

Once reranked, top-ranked candidates pass into the staging pipeline (section 6.3.3), where their provenance and authority are verified before injection into the agent’s context payload. Even an optimally ranked evidence set, however, provides bounded guarantees. Retrieving the r18 implementation and a passing reproducer confirms local syntax and isolated test execution, but it cannot prove that all concurrent caller paths remain race-free. Validating broader system properties requires corroborating records, such as runtime execution traces and multi-threaded test outcomes, that no single ranked list assembles on its own.

6.7 Multi-Hop Retrieval

In the connection-pool repair, no single ranked passage delivers all the evidence the fix requires, because the core implementation in src/pool.py, the waiter-notification test, and the recorded test execution logs answer distinct questions. Examining the pool implementation in isolation cannot reveal which execution paths the test actually exercised, and inspecting a passing test log leaves unanswered whether the change preserved wider public interface guarantees. Multi-hop retrieval addresses this gap by traversing explicit relationships between evidence items, assembling an explanatory chain that no individual passage’s retrieval score could justify alone.

Implementing multi-hop retrieval does not necessarily demand a dedicated graph database. An agent can inspect a retrieved passage, extract an unresolved reference, and issue a targeted tool call to fetch the next source artifact. Alternatively, an execution runtime can resolve known references ahead of time before invoking the model. Precomputing and maintaining explicit entity relationships pays off only when downstream queries reuse those links frequently enough to amortize the parsing and indexing overhead.

6.7.1 Evidence paths

Handing the model separate snippets from r17 test logs, tests/test_pool.py, and src/pool.py without recording how they connect leaves the agent to guess which test exercised which method and under which revision. An explicit evidence path instead chains those records together. It starts at the recorded failure of test_waiter_notified_on_return under revision r17 (scoped by its test revision and runtime environment). From there, the traversal traces from the test assertion in tests/test_pool.py directly into the pool operations exercised by the waiter-return scenario in src/pool.py. It then compares those source regions against the candidate patch in revision r18 and its corresponding reproducer execution. Public interface contracts and unrelated callers remain isolated for subsequent targeted queries.

The edges along this path carry distinct semantics. A link from a test run to a commit hash binds an outcome to an exact observed artifact. A static symbol reference in test code points only to an implementation entity awaiting inspection. A diff between revisions isolates textual modifications between snapshots. A robust retrieval payload preserves these labeled edge semantics alongside precise source locations and commit hashes. The runtime must also expose unresolved or broken links, such as a missing test log, an ambiguous dynamic dispatch target, or a traversal halted by a token budget. A static evidence path highlights plausible candidate root causes, but it cannot prove that those specific statements executed during the failing run, nor does it guarantee that all contributing dependencies were discovered.

6.7.2 Maintained code graphs

Following every file that merely imports src/pool.py floods a multi-hop search with dozens of irrelevant modules, whereas a flat symbol table cannot trace how a value mutated in one method reaches a waiter queue in another. To support selective reasoning over software repositories, a code property graph unifies abstract syntax trees, control-flow graphs, and program-dependence graphs into a single directed graph of program entities connected by typed edges (Yamaguchi et al. 2014). The edge types most relevant to software agents include syntactic containment and declaration, call edges connecting call sites to function targets, data-dependence edges tracking value definitions to their uses, type inheritance, and module-level imports.

Yamaguchi, Fabian, Nico Golde, Daniel Arp, and Konrad Rieck. 2014. “Modeling and Discovering Vulnerabilities with Code Property Graphs.” 2014 IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy, 590–604.

Edge typing is what makes multi-hop graph traversal selective rather than combinatorial.
