Restricting the walk to call and data-dependence edges traces directly from test_waiter_notified_on_return through the pool’s resource-return method to the state it mutates, ignoring module-import edges that fan out into unrelated files. The entry point for such a traversal, the seed node, can be resolved via an exact symbol lookup, an initial ranked passage retrieval, or an explicit file and line coordinate from an execution trace.

However, an edge is only as reliable as its underlying extraction mechanism. Lexical parsing reliably identifies declared syntax. Resolving cross-file call targets requires complete type information and build configuration. Dynamic execution traces observe only the concrete paths taken during a specific invocation. Code graphs must therefore track provenance on every edge, ensuring the runtime never presents a statically inferred call as an observed runtime event. Furthermore, maintaining these graphs introduces ongoing invalidation overhead as the codebase evolves. A local edit to a method signature might invalidate caller graphs and dependent summaries across multiple repositories, whereas modifying an internal comment leaves structural edges unchanged. When source artifacts shift, any relationship whose underlying evidence has changed must be pruned or marked stale according to the update contracts established in section 6.9.

6.7.3 Bounded expansion

Even on a typed code graph, an unconstrained walk out from test_waiter_notified_on_return can loop indefinitely around mutually recursive helpers or fan out through a shared logging utility into dozens of callers, exhausting the downstream context window. Traversal routines therefore require strict operational guards: filtering permitted edge types, restricting traversal direction, capping maximum search depth, and bounding the total number of visited nodes. Tracking visited entity identifiers is essential to prevent cyclic loops from triggering infinite expansion. Even with structural bounds, context packing limits remain binding because a small handful of large code or documentation nodes can easily overrun the prompt budget.

Within these operational boundaries, the runtime can prioritize candidate nodes using semantic relevance, edge type affinities, or graph-centrality algorithms like Personalized PageRank (Page et al. 1999), which concentrates random-walk probability around the initial seed nodes. However, applying a naive score threshold across candidate nodes risks fragmenting the evidence chain. Preserving a coherent explanation often requires retaining an intermediate node with a lower individual query score to maintain topological connectivity between the seed and high-value leaf evidence, while compensating by pruning non-essential nodes elsewhere.

Page, Lawrence, Sergey Brin, Rajeev Motwani, and Terry Winograd. 1999. The PageRank Citation Ranking: Bringing Order to the Web. Nos. 1999-66. Stanford InfoLab.

↪ The personalized PageRank update and its unique fixed point are developed in PageRank symbol graphing for repository maps.

One more level of depth turns a comfortable fit into an overflow.

Depth, more than node size, decides whether a neighborhood fits the context budget, because every hop multiplies the expansion frontier by the branching factor. Notebook 6.3 counts an exact tree to show how quickly one extra level turns a comfortable payload into an overflow.

Napkin Math 6.3: Graph expansion and the evidence budget
Problem: Can the complete neighborhood around one seed fit the chapter’s 6,144-token retrieval allowance?

Variables: Suppose the neighborhood is a tree with exactly 3 children per internal node, explored through depth 3, with the seed at depth zero and no repeated nodes. Each complete node record occupies 180 serialized tokens, including its local provenance. Reserve 100 tokens total for edge descriptions and the result envelope. All counts are hypothetical fixed serialization sizes.

Math: Counting the seed and all levels gives the geometric sum \(1 + 3 + 9 + 27 = (3^{4}-1)/(3-1) =\) 40 nodes, in general \((\text{children}^{\text{depth}+1}-1)/(\text{children}-1)\). Staging them requires 40 \(\times\) 180 \(+\) 100 \(=\) 7,300 tokens, exceeding the allowance. With the same envelope reservation, the maximum number of complete equal-sized nodes is \(\lfloor(6{,}144 - 100)/180\rfloor =\) 33.

Stopping one level earlier, at depth 2, stages all 13 nodes in 2,440 tokens.

Systems insight: Depth dominates traversal cost because every additional hop multiplies the expansion frontier by the graph’s branching factor. A single extra level separates an evidence payload that comfortably fits the context window from one that overflows the allocation. Traversal engines must bound depth and filter edge types before ranking nodes. Within those limits, scoring should prioritize nodes that preserve path connectivity rather than greedily selecting disconnected high-scoring fragments.

Production code graphs contain clustered communities, cycles, and heavily skewed degree distributions. Consequently, calculating reachable nodes by raising an average branching factor to a traversal depth typically overestimates distinct reachable entities while underestimating the local search work required to evaluate candidate edges. When an evidence path exceeds the staging budget, the runtime must either narrow the query scope, return a truncated subgraph accompanied by an explicit continuation cursor, or defer downstream expansion to subsequent interactive agent turns. Under the absence rule, any traversal halted by an engineering limit must be explicitly reported as partial rather than exhausted.

6.7.4 Community summaries as derived memory

Not every agent question resolves to a single evidence path. High-level architectural queries often span entire subsystems, for example how connection pooling, HTTP request dispatching, and transaction lifecycles interact across a service framework. Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) addresses these broader questions by constructing an entity knowledge graph and precomputing hierarchical summaries over densely connected subgraphs, or communities (Edge et al. 2024). Unsupervised community detection7 clusters related nodes based on network topology. However, whether a detected topological cluster corresponds to a true architectural boundary, and whether its generated natural-language summary remains technically accurate, are separate verification concerns.

Edge, Darren, Ha Trinh, Newman Cheng, Joshua Bradley, Alex Chao, Apurva Mody, Steven Truitt, and Jonathan Larson. 2024. “From Local to Global: A Graph RAG Approach to Query-Focused Summarization.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2404.16130.

7 Community Detection: GraphRAG partitions its entity graph using the Leiden algorithm, which refines modularity-based clustering to ensure that every detected community is well-separated and internally connected (Traag et al. 2019). Modularity optimization rewards groups with higher internal edge density than an equivalent random network. A detected community reflects entity co-occurrence density within the extracted corpora; whether that topological cluster corresponds to a logical module boundary recognized by system maintainers must be verified against actual source architecture.

Traag, Vincent A., Ludo Waltman, and Nees Jan van Eck. 2019. “From Louvain to Leiden: Guaranteeing Well-Connected Communities.” Scientific Reports 9 (1). https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-019-41695-z.

A derived summary amortizes token costs by synthesizing scattered facts into a concise artifact, but it remains a lossy, secondary representation. Every generated summary must retain bidirectional links to its source evidence and record the precise artifact revisions and operating parameters it describes. It must also inherit the most restrictive access label among its underlying inputs, so that a summary built partly from records restricted to one tenant is itself restricted to that tenant. Detailed execution plans should inspect the authoritative underlying records; the summary serves primarily as an indexed navigational map.

↪ How labels propagate through summaries and other transformations under an information-flow policy is developed in Label propagation through transformations.

For instance, an automated repair summary might record that revision r18 passed test_waiter_notified_on_return under a specific thread-concurrency configuration. Because an observation is scoped to its tested configuration, this fact cannot be generalized into an assertion that “the connection pool is thread-safe.” Moreover, precomputed summaries incur ongoing invalidation debt. Persisting an explicit dependency manifest between source files, detected communities, and generated summaries turns invalidation into a traversal of recorded dependencies rather than a guess. Any code modification flags all dependent summaries (and any higher-level rollups derived from them) as dirty. The runtime can continue to serve a dirty summary as a labeled historical snapshot until an offline job or on-demand trigger recompiles or invalidates it.

Checkpoint 6.2: Choosing and bounding related evidence

Apply these retrieval strategies to the running connection-pool repair.

Deciding whether to retain a detailed path explanation or a high-level subsystem summary is a write-policy decision rather than a search mechanic. That policy dictates the fidelity, staleness, and cost of the evidence that every subsequent agent session can retrieve.

6.8 The Memory Write Path

Retrieval can return only what the system has retained. An assistant that carries the directive “no meetings before 10:00” strictly within its current context window loses that constraint the moment the session terminates. Persisting the preference into long-term storage solves immediate conversational volatility, but it introduces the consistency challenge previewed in section 6.2. When the user later moves the boundary to 11:00, the runtime must ensure the earlier record stops governing new scheduling decisions without corrupting the historical audit trail.

The distinction between authoritative sources and derived records governs writes just as it governed reads (principle 6). A user’s explicit directive provides direct evidence of an intent within a specific operational scope. An agent’s internal paraphrase constitutes a lossy derived representation. An automated rationale explaining why the user dislikes morning meetings is an ungrounded secondary inference. Storing all three artifacts as interchangeable assertions flattens evidentiary weight, depriving downstream retrieval pipelines of the ability to distinguish verified user intent from speculative model summaries.

Definition 6.2: Memory write policy

A memory write policy is the runtime rule governing which proposed records enter durable storage, what claims and operational scopes they carry, and how the system subsequently corrects, supersedes, revalidates, expires, or deletes those records and their downstream derivations.

  1. Significance: Every admitted record becomes a candidate for retrieval in subsequent agent sessions. An unverified lesson admitted into storage can degrade the execution accuracy of every future task that retrieves it. The write path provides the most economical barrier against memory pollution because admission checks execute once per write rather than amortizing filtering costs across thousands of subsequent retrieval queries.
  2. Distinction: Storage retention preserves raw bytes in a database or index. The memory write policy governs the operational authority of those bytes, defining what claims an entry can justify and when it must stop influencing execution decisions.
  3. Common pitfall: Deduplicating writes based purely on embedding similarity, which erroneously discards near-identical updates (such as a revised meeting time or flipped boolean flag) as redundant duplicates.

6.8.1 Record scope and evidence

A user says “no meetings before 10:00” while looking at their team calendar in New York, and the agent stores the bare rule "no meetings before 10:00". Next month, the agent rejects a 9:30 London client call on the user’s travel calendar because the stored record never captured which calendar, which time zone, or which consent boundary authorized the write. Every candidate memory record requires a concrete provenance tuple: an identifiable owner, an intended operational role, a bounded applicability scope, and verifiable backing evidence. In addition, the runtime must enforce explicit consent boundaries: permission to inspect user messages during active reasoning does not grant permission to commit those details into durable storage.

Software repair trajectories generate a different class of evidence. For example, test execution records showing a failure at revision r17 and a pass at revision r18 represent immutable historical observations, but neither result justifies an unconditioned assertion about the codebase. A synthesized rule such as “inspect the return path when a waiter remains blocked” represents a heuristic hypothesis derived from that sequence, not a proven invariant. Table 7 formalizes these distinctions by categorizing how different memory records bind later execution.

These record kinds impose distinct demands across different operational archetypes. The research agent and support agent stress the memory write pipeline in different ways than the repair agent does.

Lighthouse 6.1: Persistent memory by archetype
Table 8 sets the three workloads side by side, from the record each one keeps to the policy it leans on.

Systems insight: The repair agent’s memory hazard comes from its own writes, the research agent’s from other people’s text, and the support agent’s from time. One retrieval contract serves all three, and the write policy is where they diverge.

6.8.2 Admission before deduplication

When a model can commit notes directly to durable storage without a runtime check, or when the write pipeline deduplicates candidates by cosine similarity before inspecting their claims, a speculative guess enters the store as fact while a preference revision from “no meetings before 10:00” to “no meetings before 11:00” is discarded as a near-duplicate. Even when an architecture allows the language model to manage its active context by paging memory entries explicitly (Packer et al. 2023), context paging controls only working set occupancy; durable admission remains the exclusive responsibility of the storage runtime. The admission gate validates write permissions, confirms that the target store allows persistent retention for the active session, classifies the record type, and verifies that attached evidence satisfies the schema before any deduplication check runs. Structural schema validation checks required fields such as timestamps and provenance identifiers.

Semantic fidelity, the requirement that the proposed record asserts nothing absent from the raw transcript, needs a check of its own, and that check is a verifier with error rates like any other. A deterministic check can validate a constrained structured input against the proposed record’s owner, relation, value, and scope. Finding “11:00” in free-form text establishes only its occurrence; “11:00 applies only on Fridays” does not support an unrestricted scheduling preference. For free-text summaries, a calibrated model-based judge is one fallible fidelity check, and its false acceptance rate (How base rates limit check precision) measures how often unsupported claims pass that check into durable storage, where every later session can retrieve them. Generating a summary and establishing that it is faithful are different events, which is the verification asymmetry (principle 3) applied to memory admission. Sensitive data (such as credentials and private identifiers) demands strict classification and scrubbing policies. Regular expression filters miss unstructured disclosures that match no standard secret pattern. When an agent proposes an interpretive conclusion lacking direct observational backing, the admission gate must either reject the candidate or downgrade it to an explicitly tagged hypothesis, preventing it from masquerading as a validated preference or concrete observation.

Only after passing admission validation does the runtime evaluate whether the candidate record inserts a new entry, updates an existing record, or duplicates existing knowledge. Vector similarity in an embedding space is useful for candidate retrieval, but it cannot resolve write semantics. Because distance thresholds would treat the 10:00-to-11:00 revision as an uninformative repeat, deduplication and update routing must match on entity identity, scope, and record kind first.

When an admitted record targets an existing entity under an identical scope, the runtime executes supersession. The runtime creates a newly versioned record and marks the prior entry as superseded. This terminates the prior record’s eligibility for active decision-making while preserving it in historical logs for auditing and provenance tracing. When scopes differ (for example, if the 11:00 restriction applies only to Fridays), both records remain valid within their respective domains. When scopes overlap ambiguously, the runtime must flag an unresolved conflict or prompt the user for clarification. Heuristically synthesizing conflicting records into a fuzzy compromise produces brittle, ungrounded behavior. Systems must also distinguish an in-place correction from a true state transition. Fixing a transcription error that corrupted “10:00” repairs the fidelity of an existing record, whereas accepting an 11:00 update documents an authentic change in preference.

Concurrent updates demand explicit coordination primitives at the storage layer. A proposed supersession must carry the base version identifier against which it was generated. The store then evaluates and applies the update atomically through a compare-and-swap or conditional write. This is the same optimistic validation that context engineering applied to tool preconditions, and it closes the race inherent in separate read and write phases. Optimistic validation suits memory writes particularly well. Two sessions seldom revise the same preference concurrently, so conflicts are rare, and a model call lasting several seconds makes a pessimistic lock impractical to hold across the invocation that proposes the write. When a collision does occur, the runtime must abort the write, reload the latest state, and re-evaluate the update against an explicit conflict resolution policy. Handing the conflict back to the model to blend the two states without transactional guarantees invites silent corruption.

↩︎ Optimistic validation, which checks at commit that the state a decision read is unchanged and aborts otherwise, is established in Detecting change and bounding uncertainty.

6.8.3 Recorded observations and derived lessons

If an agent waits for a reflective summary and vector embedding to finish before recording a tool’s raw output, a timeout or crash in the summarization model loses the hard evidence of what the tool actually did. When an agent executes an action, the runtime immediately writes the raw observation (the execution context, task ID, source revision, tool parameters, and raw output status) to durable storage. Only after this initial write does the runtime schedule background pipelines for summarization, embedding generation, and heuristic reflection, keeping costly model calls off the synchronous tool path. Generating reflective summaries has demonstrated substantial empirical gains in autonomous error recovery (Shinn et al. 2023). The resulting verbal reflections must nonetheless enter the store strictly as generated hypotheses (table 7), with explicit foreign-key links to the raw observations from which they were induced.

Write acknowledgments must explicitly report the durability boundary achieved. Systems must distinguish among three distinct guarantees: buffered in memory (queued in an ephemeral local ring buffer), committed to durable storage (flushed to non-volatile disk or replicated across storage nodes under the durability contract), and indexed for search (visible to vector and keyword retrieval engines). Merely enqueuing an event into an in-memory worker queue provides zero crash durability. If a task requires strict persistence guarantees before taking non-reversible external actions, the runtime must block on storage confirmation rather than proceeding optimistically. In addition, background indexing and reflection pipelines consume significant accelerator cycles and storage I/O. System schedulers must explicitly account for these background workloads to prevent asynchronous memory indexing from starving user-facing interactive inference.

6.8.4 Revalidation, expiration, and deletion

Once a record is committed, its status changes as its supporting evidence and operational context evolve. The superseded 10:00 preference and the r17 test failure both remain accurate historical records after they lose eligibility for current decisions, while an unconfirmed scheduling rule retrieved across 30 runs without complaint is thirty times reused, not thirty times verified. Retaining historical snapshots is a deliberate architectural policy choice, and keeping active records trustworthy over time requires explicit rules for revalidation, expiration, and deletion.

Revalidation refreshes the empirical support for an existing record. Asking the user to confirm that the 11:00 scheduling boundary remains active provides authoritative fresh evidence, whereas repeated retrieval across dozens of runs indicates only frequent usage and adds no new support. Consequently, storage schemas must maintain distinct timestamps for record creation, last access, and last authoritative confirmation. Similarly, revalidating an automated diagnosis or heuristic rule derived from revision r18 requires evaluating it against the current source code tree and active test environment, rather than treating age-based decay as a proxy for correctness.

Expiration revokes a record’s eligibility for decision-making once an expiration timestamp passes or a defined system condition is triggered. An expired temporary directive, such as an out-of-office scheduling window, must not govern subsequent actions without explicit reconfirmation. Some memory systems rank records by similarity multiplied by a recency factor that shrinks with age. Penalizing an expired record this way is inadequate, because if a query has few close semantic matches, an expired record with a penalized score can still surface in the top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) retrieval set and contaminate the prompt. Robust write paths enforce hard filtering, partitioning or tagging expired records so that operational retrieval filters exclude them unconditionally while retaining the payload for compliance auditing.

Records built from a user’s own data carry an obligation that records about source code do not, because the person they describe has a say in whether and how long they persist. Permission to read a message during reasoning does not grant permission to retain it (section 6.8.1). Each admitted user record should therefore carry the consent under which it was admitted and a retention period after which it must be deleted rather than merely excluded from retrieval. A store can honor a withdrawal of consent only if it can list every record that describes the user, derived summaries included, together with the consent recorded for each. Withdrawal of consent, an elapsed retention period, and an explicit deletion request all start the same deletion procedure.

Deletion addresses the physical and logical lifecycle of the stored data. When a user revokes consent or asks to delete a preference, purging the primary database row is only the initial step. Stale representations frequently linger in secondary indexes, dense vector stores, consolidated episodic summaries, key-value caches, and pre-staged prompt templates managed by the runtime. The key-value case is easy to miss, because a cached prefix that contains the deleted preference still matches any request that repeats those tokens, and a prefix match certifies identical tokens, not current facts. The runtime must therefore evict that prefix or change the prompt so it no longer matches. A comprehensive deletion procedure must identify every downstream derivative, revoke its retrieval eligibility immediately, and schedule background reclamation or re-indexing under the governing data retention policy. To bridge the latency between request receipt and asynchronous garbage collection, systems write the storage counterpart of the marker that context invalidation places in a prompt. This tombstone8 is a durable exclusion record that filters the entity out of all active query paths before physical purging completes.

8 Death Certificates: Distributed replicated databases encountered this challenge decades ago. If a replica silently deletes an entry, a delayed update arriving from a partitioned peer appears to be fresh data and resurrects the deleted record. Demers et al. (1987) resolved this in epidemic replication by writing an explicit “death certificate” that propagates through the cluster and supersedes older versions. In an agent memory architecture, lagging asynchronous indexers, pipeline queues, and restored snapshots function just like stale replicas, making durable tombstone markers necessary prior to physical deletion.

Demers, Alan, Dan Greene, Carl Hauser, Wes Irish, and John Larson. 1987. “Epidemic Algorithms for Replicated Database Maintenance.” Proceedings of the Sixth Annual ACM Symposium on Principles of Distributed Computing, 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/41840.41841.

↩︎ Why a prefix-cache hit verifies identical tokens rather than fresh evidence is established in Prefix compatibility.

↩︎ Tombstones as masking records that log-structured storage engines keep until compaction, and their contrast with an advisory notice in a prompt, are established in Removing, replacing, or appending.

A deletion receipt must specify the exact boundary of guarantees achieved. Immutable cold backups, out-of-band external audit logs, and in-flight API payloads dispatched to third-party model providers lie outside the synchronous deletion perimeter. Deleting a record from runtime memory cannot retract a prompt token that an external foundation model has already processed, nor can it un-schedule a calendar invite already sent across an enterprise network. What the runtime can and must guarantee is that every internal index, cache, and prompt assembly buffer within its trust domain immediately ceases to use the deleted entity in future reasoning cycles.

6.8.5 Consolidation

After a dozen debugging sessions on an asynchronous runtime, retrieving forty separate stack traces and raw test logs overflows the retrieval budget before the model sees the pattern connecting them. Consolidation compresses clusters of related, fine-grained records into structured, high-level representations optimized for future retrieval, such as an architectural summary documenting which notification paths were evaluated, which revisions broke invariants, and which hypotheses remain open. Autonomous agent architectures frequently employ background workers to synthesize episodic observations into higher-level reflections (Park et al. 2023). From a systems perspective, every consolidated reflection is a generated lesson (table 7). Clustering records by embedding proximity helps identify candidate groups for consolidation, but vector similarity alone cannot establish causal relationships. Verifying that two failures share an underlying defect requires structural evidence that semantic proximity cannot supply.

When generating consolidated records, the write pipeline must preserve provenance graphs, explicitly recording references to the constituent raw records, boundary conditions, and any observed counterexamples. Synthesizing multiple successful execution traces into an unconditioned, global procedure risks overgeneralizing domain-specific behavior. Retaining the underlying source observations alongside the consolidated abstraction also remains a deliberate architectural decision. Consolidated summaries minimize prompt tokens during inference, but stripping the raw logs removes the granular execution traces necessary for deep auditing and subsequent re-indexing.

6.8.6 Self-reinforcing errors

A faulty diagnostic guess that is copied into an episode summary, absorbed into a weekly milestone report, and ultimately consolidated into a cross-project heuristic still rests entirely on the original faulty inference. Under formal evidentiary rules, repeated retrieval and re-summarization provide zero independent validation, yet each consolidation cycle risks treating the stored conjecture as an established premise and amplifying it across successive generations of context. Memory poisoning is the persistence of an unverified or withdrawn claim through the records derived from it. To prevent it, the storage engine must maintain rigorous dependency provenance across all derived artifacts. When an operator or verification pass invalidates a poisoned record, the invalidation must cascade through the entire dependency graph, retracting or marking as suspect every downstream summary and derived lesson. Merely down-ranking or tombstoning the root record is insufficient if derivative summaries continue circulating through retrieval indexes. Finally, even a fully validated, un-poisoned lesson functions merely as a candidate action suggestion. The execution engine must still enforce strict runtime authorization policies and environmental preconditions whenever an agent attempts to act on retrieved memories.

Checkpoint 6.3: Following a preference through its lifecycle

A user changes “no meetings before 10:00” to “no meetings before 11:00” for the same calendar and later requests deletion of the preference. An index and a session summary still contain the original value.

The memory write path establishes what a stored record claims, what evidence supports it, and when its operational validity ends. Those decisions reach the agent, however, only through derived indexes and caches that are rebuilt after the store changes, and until a lagging index catches up it can still answer with the record the write path just retired.

6.9 Storage Invalidation

The connection-pool repair has reached revision r18, yet a subsequent search can still return an excerpt of src/pool.py indexed at r17. Consider how a single stale read unravels an otherwise systematic debugging trajectory:

  1. At turn 12, the agent edits the return path in src/pool.py, and the workspace edit tool confirms the updated file at r18.
  2. At turn 13, a search for the notification logic retrieves the r17 chunk, which still contains the buggy line replaced in the previous turn, without a version label.
  3. At turn 14, the model infers that its earlier modification was lost and reissues the edit. The edit tool rejects the request because the target snippet no longer exists in the current file.
  4. At turn 15, the model confronts conflicting signals: a confirmed write, an excerpt contradicting that write, and a failed re-edit. It concludes that the workspace filesystem is corrupted and begins issuing speculative, wide-ranging edits. Each action burns context tokens and tool budget while driving the repository further from a reviewed state.

The unlabeled stale read initiates this failure by presenting an accurate historical excerpt as current state. The conflicting evidence does not establish filesystem corruption. A direct read of the current source could expose the mismatch. Model reasoning cannot reliably compensate for a retrieval layer that conceals which version its evidence describes, and each turn the trace wasted lengthens the horizon over which per-turn errors compound (equation 1.1). Maintaining a persistent index therefore forces a clean separation between two operations that an ephemeral staged prompt naturally couples: withholding obsolete records and generating their replacements.

Definition 6.3: Storage invalidation

Storage invalidation is the exclusion of a derived record (an index entry, vector, summary, or cached result) from current-state retrieval once a relevant change to its source makes it inapplicable, independent of when a replacement is built.

  1. Significance: Coordinating exclusion with the source commit prevents known-obsolete entries from remaining eligible while their replacements are built. Notebook 6.4 compares the additional metadata and indexing work, separately from the common durable commit and coordination costs. In the turn-by-turn r17-to-r18 trace from turns 12 through 15, one unlabeled stale read cost three turns of the repair’s budget.
  2. Distinction: Rebuilding synthesizes a replacement representation, whereas invalidation merely suppresses the obsolete one. This ensures that any query arriving during the rebuild window receives either an authoritative direct read or an explicit incomplete result rather than stale data.
  3. Common pitfall: Tracking staleness with an ephemeral dirty flag that clears automatically when a background job finishes. If an uncoordinated indexing worker completes late or a retrieval gateway restarts without durable tombstone state, obsolete entries can be erroneously readmitted.

6.9.1 Source generations

Relying on filesystem modification timestamps (mtime) to detect stale index entries fails when clock skew, branch switches, or sub-millisecond edits leave two revisions with indistinguishable timestamps. To guarantee freshness, an index entry must explicitly record the source generation from which it was derived: a monotonic version identity defined by the storage system’s write contract. In the connection-pool repair, r17 and r18 denote successive repository states. Once the runtime establishes r18 as the active generation, any query seeking current code must systematically discard entries tagged with r17.

Deciding which derived records an edit affects is the dependency tracking that context invalidation applied to staged observations, now applied to records that persist in the store and its indexes. What is new at this layer is that generations make the tracking durable across sessions, and that some derived records cannot be patched in place. Data dependencies often span beyond individual files. A raw code excerpt depends solely on its source file revision. A call-graph edge depends on multiple source files alongside the static-analysis configuration. An episodic summary depends on the tool outputs and diagnostic logs it consolidates. When an underlying artifact changes, the runtime must invalidate all affected downstream records while keeping unrelated entries intact. If the dependency graph is coarse or incomplete, the runtime must conservatively widen the invalidation boundary or explicitly report retrieval uncertainty. A missing dependency link must never be treated as proof of validity.

↩︎ Tracking which claims depend on which observations, and propagating invalidation through that record, is established in Tracking claim dependencies.

From a systems standpoint, every retrieval index is a materialized view over base data, a problem relational database management systems have addressed for decades. Classical incremental view maintenance computes updates to a view algebraically from delta changes in base relations, bypassing the need to re-evaluate the full query (Blakeley et al. 1986; Gupta and Mumick 1995). Unstructured retrieval indexes, however, resist algebraic delta maintenance. Under fixed-size chunking schemes, inserting even two lines near the beginning of a source file shifts the token boundaries of every subsequent chunk. Because deep neural embedding models are non-linear, an updated vector cannot be synthesized from a vector subtraction and a text diff. Consequently, changed text requires discarding existing entries and running end-to-end forward passes to re-embed the new chunks. Practical retrieval architectures therefore decouple invalidation, a metadata exclusion coordinated with the source commit, from index rebuilding, which consumes substantial compute and network latency.

Beyond source generations, an embedding record must also encode the model identifier, tokenization pipeline, and preprocessing hyperparameters that generated it. A valid source generation confirms only that the underlying text is up to date. It does not indicate whether the vector remains compatible with the active retrieval model and query encoder.

6.9.2 Source and index coordination

If the workspace commits r18 and publishes an asynchronous invalidation event on a message queue, a retrieval query arriving two milliseconds later can reach the index gateway before the queue message does and receive r17 as current state. Avoiding that race requires a coordinated protocol among the storage coordinator, the indexing pipeline, and the retrieval gateway. This protocol assumes that the agent runtime governs workspace modifications. It also assumes that the retrieval gateway can query an authoritative source generation along with its content. Finally, it assumes that writes, index publications, and query admission share a coordination boundary, such as an atomic database transaction, a conditional write to a strongly consistent store whose replicas agree on a single commit order, or a local mutex. During an in-flight write, the gateway either holds queries within a tight latency deadline or marks the affected file scope as temporarily unavailable. A message queue or publish-subscribe broker alone cannot enforce these properties, because delivering a message is a separate event from committing the write it announces. The coordination sequence orchestrates a source modification while a straggling index worker remains active:

  1. The source coordinator commits the patch as generation r18 and atomically updates the active generation watermark for admission checks. Here a watermark is a position marker, the highest generation the gateway treats as current, not one of the compaction thresholds of context engineering. This prevents a visibility split where newly committed code is readable from disk while an out-of-band index registry continues to treat r17 as current.
  2. A subsequent query for src/pool.py retrieves candidate vectors derived from r17 from the background index. The retrieval gateway checks their embedded generation tag against the active watermark (r18) and suppresses them. This exclusion signals index lag rather than a missing file or a failed edit.
  3. An indexing job started prior to the commit finishes late. During index publication, the worker checks its captured generation within the coordination boundary. Recognizing that generation r18 has superseded its input, the coordinator discards the stale output or redirects it to an archival store.
  4. A fresh worker generates replacement vectors from an r18 snapshot. It publishes them conditionally, committing the batch only if the workspace generation and index configuration remain unchanged throughout the indexing window.
  5. Inbound queries continuously evaluate candidate generations against the active watermark. Publishing r18 records never reactivates older r17 entries, and background garbage collectors reclaim retired vectors on an independent schedule.

Publication validation and index installation must occur as a single atomic operation, because checking generation validity first and writing later introduces a time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) race condition that allows concurrent edits to invalidate the payload. In-memory and durable tombstones record excluded generations while background workers rebuild representations. Because query admission enforces generation checks on every lookup, old candidates remain excluded even if temporary dirty flags clear prematurely. Similarly, deleting a source file installs a versioned tombstone, preventing delayed workers from accidentally resurrecting an obsolete record from an earlier snapshot.

Because most commits modify only a few files, replacing the entire index on every commit would waste work. Entries for files untouched by r18 remain accurate even though they were indexed at r17 or earlier, whereas entries for src/pool.py become obsolete at r18. Source generations capture this per-entry validity in a compact, interval-based form. Each derived entry \(\DerivedMemoryEntry\) carries a valid lifetime \([\MemoryEntryBirthGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry), \MemoryEntryDeathGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry))\), where \(\MemoryEntryDeathGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry)\) remains open (\(\infty\)) as long as the underlying source scope is untouched and is set to the superseding generation (such as r18 for src/pool.py’s r17 records) when an edit modifies or deletes that scope. A query evaluating workspace generation \(\QueryWorkspaceGeneration\) admits candidate entry \(\DerivedMemoryEntry\) if and only if

\[ \text{IsValid}(\DerivedMemoryEntry, \QueryWorkspaceGeneration) = \big(\MemoryEntryBirthGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry) \le \QueryWorkspaceGeneration < \MemoryEntryDeathGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry)\big). \tag{4}\]

For current-state execution, the runtime sets \(\QueryWorkspaceGeneration\) to the active workspace generation. For historical diagnostics, such as an agent reviewing why an execution failed at revision r17, the query sets \(\QueryWorkspaceGeneration\) to that historical revision. Subject to retention policies, equation 4 serves both use cases uniformly, admitting src/pool.py’s [r17, r18) entries for an r17 replay while excluding them for r18.

Exclusion solves freshness, but it introduces an efficiency trade-off that compounds over time. Suppressed records continue to occupy memory. In an HNSW graph, tombstoned nodes remain embedded in their neighbors’ adjacency lists. Because greedy graph traversal continues to visit and evaluate these dead nodes before filtering them out at the admission boundary, a search with a fixed beam width explores fewer live items, degrading overall recall. To bound that loss, a background compaction process tracks the ratio of tombstoned records, \(\TombstonedRecordFraction = \lvert \TombstonedNodeSet\rvert/\lvert \IndexGraphNodeSet\rvert\). When this ratio crosses a tuned threshold, the system builds a compacted graph: it purges unreferenced records, recomputes neighbor connections among surviving nodes, rebuilds navigation entry points, and atomically swaps the compacted index into memory. This workflow adopts the compaction design of log-structured merge-trees (O’Neil et al. 1996), balancing query latency and retrieval recall against the compute cost of background rebuilds. This is compaction in the storage-engine sense, rewriting a structure without its dead entries, not the summarization that context compaction performs on a prompt.

This coordination contract must remain robust against system restarts and uncoordinated writers. When a retrieval gateway restarts, it must reconstruct its authoritative generation state from durable storage before accepting queries. An empty in-memory tombstone table must never be treated as evidence that the index is fresh. If external tools or developers modify files without routing through the runtime coordinator, filesystem watcher events may arrive out of order or be dropped. In response, the gateway must fall back to direct, version-validated reads against the filesystem. If it cannot verify whether an indexed chunk matches disk before its query deadline expires, it must flag the result envelope as unverified rather than returning potentially stale data.

Replicated retrieval topologies require explicit read-your-writes9 guarantees, ensuring that an agent always observes the outcomes of its own preceding actions (Terry et al. 1994). When an agent issues a search following an edit, the query carries the log sequence number (LSN) corresponding to that write. A replica whose replay position (the last log entry it has applied) lags that LSN must pause execution within a bounded wait window. If the replica does not catch up before the deadline elapses, the gateway either routes the request to an authoritative primary or returns an explicit partial-status response.

9 Session Guarantees: The Bayou project established four foundational client-centric guarantees for weakly consistent distributed storage: read your writes, monotonic reads, writes follow reads, and monotonic writes (Terry et al. 1994). An agentic system requires more than simple read-your-writes behavior. For example, if an agent reads r18 from an up-to-date replica and subsequently retrieves r17 from a lagging node on the next step, the system violates monotonic reads, and the agent’s view of workspace history moves backward.

Terry, Douglas B., Alan J. Demers, Karin Petersen, Mike J. Spreitzer, Marvin M. Theimer, and Brent B. Welch. 1994. “Session Guarantees for Weakly Consistent Replicated Data.” Proceedings of the Third International Conference on Parallel and Distributed Information Systems, 140–49. https://doi.org/10.1109/PDIS.1994.331722.

6.9.3 Retrieval during index refresh

Suppressing the stale r17 vector for src/pool.py stops the agent from reading obsolete code, but while the r18 embedding is still building in the background, a query for the pool implementation still needs an answer. When invalidation suppresses a record during an index refresh, the gateway must return an honest assessment of available evidence. As outlined in table 9, a production retrieval layer can respond with one of three outcomes.

The appropriate fallback depends on query structure. For an explicit target path like src/pool.py, the gateway can execute an authorized direct source read, returning the live r18 text while background embedding jobs run. Conversely, a broad semantic query, such as a search for all modules affected by a modified locking interface, cannot be answered by reading a single file. Answering it accurately requires either a refreshed semantic index or comprehensive static analysis across many files. If bounded direct reads cannot resolve the query within the execution budget, the gateway must return an explicit incomplete result. Artificially inflating candidate limits (such as expanding \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) during top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) nearest-neighbor retrieval) does not solve this problem, because an unindexed record is absent from the vector index and no search depth can retrieve it.

Where to place this maintenance overhead is an explicit design choice. Synchronous re-indexing moves parsing, chunking, and embedding directly onto the write path, guaranteeing that fresh representations exist before any subsequent tool executes. Background re-indexing acknowledges writes after the required durable source commit and coordinated exclusion, allowing tools to return before rebuilding finishes while relying on the gateway to manage queries during the refresh window. This architectural trade-off depends on workspace edit frequency and whether bounded direct reads can satisfy immediate downstream queries. As notebook 6.4 quantifies, their additional indexing and local metadata work can differ by several orders of magnitude, and they also differ in what a query during the rebuild sees (figure 7). Synchronous re-indexing never exposes that interval but stalls the write, unchecked background indexing serves r17 entries as current, and background indexing with exclusion suppresses r17 and answers the query with a direct read or a partial result.

Napkin Math 6.4: Who pays for keeping the index current
Problem: The repair’s next edit modifies 8 files across 16 indexed chunks. All strategies share the same durable source-commit and coordination boundary. The exclusion costs count only incremental in-memory metadata updates and lookups, excluding durable commit and replication, coordination contention, restart reconstruction, and fallback retrieval. The stipulated per-stage costs are 3.5 ms to parse a file and 2 ms to update one chunk’s lexical postings. A local embedding batch of up to 16 chunks costs 28 ms, and a remote embedding RPC costs 150 ms. Recording an exclusion entry costs 50 ns, and checking an entry costs 30 ns. Given these costs, how much additional indexing or local metadata work does each strategy add, and which path bears it?

Math: Synchronous re-indexing halts write completion until parsing, postings updates, and vector embeddings are fully finished. Backed by a local embedding model, this pipeline adds 88 ms directly to the write path. A single batched remote embedding call increases write overhead to 210 ms, while serial remote requests per file inflate it to 1.26 s. In contrast, updating 8 local exclusion entries adds 0.4 μs of in-memory metadata work. Subsequent retrieval passes check 50 candidates against this metadata, adding 1.5 μs of local lookup work. The local re-indexing stages consume 220,000× as much time as these metadata updates. This ratio does not compare complete durable write latencies.

Systems insight: Under the coordinated generation protocol, exclusion suppresses known-obsolete entries before subsequent admission, though it does not produce fresh representations immediately. The small local metadata costs do not price the complete durable enforcement path. The computational cost of re-indexing is not eliminated; it is deferred to background workers, requiring the query gateway to manage the staleness gap using the strategies in table 9. Coordinate exclusion with the source commit. Consider synchronous re-indexing when dependent queries arrive before background pipelines can finish and bounded fallback cannot supply the required evidence.

Retrieved evidence represents the source state observed at the moment of the read. In an agentic system, a concurrent write can land before the model finishes deliberating and invokes its chosen action. Any tool call whose correctness depends on an unmodified workspace must therefore enforce an execution-time precondition, such as an optimistic version check, at the tool boundary. The storage subsystem’s core duty is to preserve and surface the exact generation metadata that this precondition requires.

↪ The atomic conditional update that enforces this version precondition at the tool boundary is developed in Conditional updates.

Saltzer, Jerome H., David P. Reed, and David D. Clark. 1984. “End-to-End Arguments in System Design.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (4): 277–88. https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402.

Source-generation checks suppress obsolete records, atomic publication prevents straggling workers from reinstating stale representations, and explicit fallback contracts clarify whether fresh evidence is available. Together, these mechanisms enforce internal storage consistency rather than task success. In accordance with the end-to-end argument (Saltzer et al. 1984), whether the r18 patch truly resolves the stalled waiter can be established only at the system boundary through integration tests executed against the real runtime environment. What the storage layer guarantees to that boundary is an uncompromised, auditable record of the exact source generation reflected in every retrieved fact.

6.10 Evaluating Memory Policies

Excluding a stale failure trace from an earlier run keeps an outdated representation out of the agent’s next request, but it does not ensure that the agent finds the fresh evidence needed to effect a repair. Conversely, a stored reflection might reproduce a recent passing test run accurately while overstating what the pass proves. Rigorous evaluation cannot treat memory as a static lookup table. It must follow information across its full lifecycle: from initial admission into persistent storage, through indexing, retrieval, and prompt staging, to the subsequent updates, supersessions, and deletions that maintain consistency as the environment evolves.

The appropriate unit of comparison is the memory policy. A memory policy encompasses what an agent writes, how it indexes and retrieves records, how it stages evidence in the prompt, and how it maintains that evidence across sessions. The retrieval index is merely one component of that policy. Component-level microbenchmarks help locate failures in search precision or index latency, but task-level outcomes ultimately decide whether the assembled policy satisfies operational acceptance criteria.

6.10.1 Cross-session policy trials

If an evaluation harness shares a single writable vector store across candidate memory policies, a reflection or reference answer written during Policy A’s run leaks into Policy B’s trial and makes Policy B appear to learn from tasks it never solved. A controlled cross-session trial begins with a fixed source snapshot and an isolated initial memory store for each policy, running identical task sequences under identical constraints on model inference, tool execution, and compute budgets. Records generated during one trial session may influence subsequent sessions within that same trial, since that cross-session behavior is what the trial measures, but they must never contaminate a parallel trial. Ground-truth reference answers, test fixtures, and evaluator annotations must remain strictly outside both writable and retrievable stores.

↪ Evaluation leakage, including solutions that persistent memory carries from one run into the next, is developed in Evaluation leakage.

The comparison in table 10 isolates the baseline benefit of raw persistence from the operational benefit of governing what persists. Across all experimental conditions, the system enforces identical access controls and action permissions. The “unchecked” condition isolates content admission alone, while all conditions experience external source modifications and user updates on the same synchronized fixture schedule.

The scheduling preference supplies a concrete lifecycle fixture: an initial preference recorded at 10:00, an explicit supersession at 11:00, and a user deletion request in a subsequent session. The evaluation tests whether the agent applies the active preference before deletion and complies with post-deletion invariants afterward. This requires inspecting every storage tier: primary vector and keyword indices, consolidated summaries, and staged prompt templates. An agent that suppresses the primary record from raw search results but still retrieves an unpruned summary containing the old preference violates its withdrawal contract. System backups or audit logs retained for regulatory compliance must also fall under an explicit data-retention policy rather than being counted as active working memory.

Similarly, an iterative software-repair fixture retains the failing run at r17, records the subsequent passing run at r18, and then challenges a future agent session to distinguish historical debugging context from the current state of the implementation without inappropriately broadening the reproducer’s scope. When source files change, the fixture tests two behaviors: a relevant source edit should trigger revalidation of dependent records, while an unrelated source edit tests whether the policy mistakenly discards valid, useful evidence.

Deterministic fixed-record probes isolate specific defects in admission filtering, retrieval ranking, or cache invalidation. Full task trials then allow policies to accumulate their own records across multi-turn interactions, causing their execution trajectories to diverge naturally over time. Evaluators must hold initial conditions and scoring rubrics constant across runs. When stochastic generation or environment variations introduce noise, evaluations must report the number of independent trials alongside the full distribution of outcomes rather than relying solely on mean performance.

6.10.2 Retrieval relevance judgments

Scoring a retriever by whether it returns the r17 failure log for a query about src/pool.py rewards the right record when the task asks for bug history and rewards a stale defect when the task asks whether r18 passes. For each test query, the benchmark must define the eligible source snapshot, the authorized access scope, and the specific records relevant to the agent’s immediate task. Evaluation fixtures must explicitly label temporal validity rather than expecting an embedding model’s similarity score to distinguish historical context from current state.

Let \(\RetrievedRecordSet_k\) be the set of distinct records returned up to cutoff \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\), and let \(\RelevantRecordSet\) be the set of labeled relevant records in the eligible snapshot. Precision at cutoff \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) is defined as

\[ \text{P}@\RetrievalCutoffCount = \frac{\lvert\RetrievedRecordSet_k \cap \RelevantRecordSet\rvert}{\RetrievalCutoffCount}, \qquad \RetrievalCutoffCount > 0. \tag{5}\]

The denominator is the target cutoff \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\), not the number of records the retriever actually returned. Unfilled result positions receive zero credit. Under this formulation, an empty result set scores zero, ensuring that a policy that underfills its staged context cannot artificially outscore a policy that retrieves a broader set of useful evidence. In contrast, calculating precision strictly over the returned records (with \(\lvert\RetrievedRecordSet_k\rvert\) in the denominator) can invert these rankings. Evaluators must explicitly declare their denominator convention and report the raw count of returned records alongside precision scores.

Recall measures the fraction of relevant evidence retrieved from the eligible store:

\[ \text{Recall}@\RetrievalCutoffCount = \frac{\lvert\RetrievedRecordSet_k \cap \RelevantRecordSet\rvert}{\lvert\RelevantRecordSet\rvert}. \tag{6}\]

When the relevant set \(\RelevantRecordSet\) is empty, recall is mathematically undefined (\(0/0\)). Evaluators should isolate unanswerable queries into a dedicated reporting cohort, assessing whether the agent correctly recognizes the absence of supporting evidence or fabricates an unsupported claim. Keeping the completion status of every evaluation query visible ensures that such boundary conditions are not swept into an aggregate average.

When surfacing an immediate answer is the priority, Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) evaluates how quickly the system finds its first hit. MRR computes the arithmetic mean of the reciprocal rank of the first relevant record across all evaluated queries, scoring zero whenever no relevant item appears within cutoff \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\). However, MRR is insensitive to whether the complete evidence required for a multi-hop reasoning chain was assembled. Multi-hop evaluation fixtures must separately verify that all required entity relationships and source versions are present.

Temporal applicability demands a separate metric. The stale-return rate is the fraction of current-state queries that return at least one record invalidated by subsequent source modifications. It measures the store one layer below the stale-support rate of context engineering (equation 4.7). Stale-return counts what retrieval hands back. Stale-support counts decisions that rest on superseded evidence once it is in context. A stale record that the runtime labels or excludes at staging therefore raises the first without raising the second. Historical retrospective queries must be evaluated and reported separately. Because a retriever that returns nothing returns no stale records, a degenerate policy that returns zero results achieves a perfect stale-return rate of zero while completely failing the task. Consequently, stale-return rates must always be reported alongside recall and evidence coverage.

6.10.3 Lifecycle outcomes and full costs

A memory policy that deletes a user preference from its primary table in 2 ms while leaving the preference inside a cached episode summary and a background embedding queue appears fast and compliant only until the next session stages the summary. In supersession and deletion trials, evaluators track whether an updated or deleted record, along with all its downstream representations (index postings, graph edges, and cached summaries), reached the declared target state within an established deadline. The benchmark logs update latency, queue backlogs, and unresolved timeouts. In this analysis, expiring a record from active retrieval and physically purging it from persistent storage are distinct operations that require distinct accounting. Describing a record loosely as “forgotten” obscures whether data was merely hidden from the read path or permanently deleted from the database.

End-to-end task acceptance remains the ultimate arbiter of system quality. The acceptance rate must be calculated across all admitted task trials, explicitly including timeouts, uncaught exceptions, and partial completions in the denominator. Satisfying an obsolete constraint is an operational failure, even if the retriever achieved high precision against a stale label set.

System resource accounting must respect the same strict evaluation boundaries. Cost accounting aggregates the end-to-end compute footprint: query generation, keyword search, dense similarity computation, cross-encoder reranking, model token usage, memory writes, offline consolidation, and background index compaction. This tally must include asynchronous background operations that do not directly block foreground user requests, as well as deferred maintenance tasks lingering at the end of the evaluation window. Dividing the total expenditure by the number of successfully completed tasks yields the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2). If a system completes zero tasks, evaluators must report total expenditure and zero successes rather than computing an undefined or misleading ratio.

Storage measurements require equally clear accounting boundaries. Benchmarks should report raw source bytes separately from auxiliary structures, including inverted index postings, dense vector tables, graph adjacency lists, version metadata, and durability replicas. A benchmark report must explicitly define whether its reported storage amplification represents auxiliary bytes divided by raw source bytes, or total stored bytes (source plus auxiliary) divided by raw source bytes. Similarly, update-latency benchmarks must compare equivalent operations: a full index rebuild against a full index rebuild, rather than comparing a complete re-indexing pass against an asynchronous tombstone lookup. Notebook 6.5 compares both metrics across two representative store architectures.

Dense vectors multiply a store’s auxiliary bytes by an order of magnitude.

Napkin Math 6.5: Why two memory stores differ by an order of magnitude
Problem: A hypothetical repository holds 50,000 files of 200 tokens each, 40 MB of source. Store A adds a BM25 index and a structural graph with 4 edges per file. Store B adds the same BM25 index plus 100-token chunks embedded as 768-dimensional vectors under an HNSW graph shaped like the one in table 5. The vectors are kept at full FP32 width, twice the FP16 width of the exact-scan corpus, and the encoder is stipulated to be slower than the local batch of notebook 6.4. How do their auxiliary bytes and re-index times compare?

Math: Store A’s auxiliary structures occupy 14.4 MB, an auxiliary ratio of 0.36× over the raw source (total ratio 1.36×). Store B’s occupy 340.8 MB, of which the vectors alone are 307.2 MB, an auxiliary ratio of 8.52× (total 9.52×). After an edit to 15 files, at stipulated throughputs for postings updates and parsing, Store A re-indexes in 34.1 ms. Store B must re-embed 30 chunks. At 50 chunks per second it needs 612 ms.

Store B carries ~24× the auxiliary bytes of Store A and takes ~18× as long to re-index.

Systems insight: Dense vectors dominate both the bytes and the maintenance time, because each chunk becomes hundreds of floating-point components produced by a model pass. Compressing the vectors attacks the byte term, and storing them at FP16 would halve the vector bytes. Only a faster encoder or fewer re-embedded chunks attacks the time term, which sets the stale window after every edit. The time ratio therefore depends on the stipulated encoder throughput and shrinks with a faster encoder, while the byte ratio does not depend on it. A dense index is worth its order of magnitude only when it raises task acceptance enough to repay that maintenance in the cost per accepted task. The live-source baseline of section 6.4 pays neither term, which is why it is the comparison every added index must beat.

Staging depth warrants independent empirical measurement because task success does not increase monotonically with the retrieval cutoff \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\). Recall@\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) (equation 6) cannot decrease as \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) increases, but every added record changes the prompt the model must use. Two effects that limit usable working context act together here. Each superficially similar but irrelevant passage adds to the softmax normalizer and so dilutes the attention the critical evidence receives (attention dilution). A larger staged set also pushes some of that evidence toward the middle of the prompt, where models use it least reliably (lost in the middle) (Liu et al. 2024). Too few records starve the decision of evidence; too many bury it. The optimal operating range depends heavily on the model’s attention architecture, task complexity, and reranker quality. This mirrors the working-set view of context (principle 4). In practice, a retrieval pipeline should configure candidate generation to maximize recall, use a precise reranker to stage only a compact working set into the prompt, and systematically sweep staging depth across evaluation trials rather than assuming a single static cutoff.

↩︎ Usable working context, with attention dilution and the lost-in-the-middle effect that bound it, is established in Usable Working Context.

Benign workload trials therefore map a multidimensional trade-off space rather than a single scalar benchmark score. A more expansive memory store retains helpful edge-case experiences, but it also accumulates obsolete or contradictory records. Conversely, a strict admission gate shields the agent from spurious lessons at the expense of rejecting valuable domain context. Varying read and write policies under identical operational budgets separates whether accepted task outcomes change because of evidence coverage, temporal applicability, lifecycle maintenance, or compute and storage costs, before testing how those same policies hold up when retrieved content is actively hostile.

6.10.4 Adversarial boundary tests

An indirect prompt injection embedded inside a fetched documentation page can trick a background reflection worker into storing an unauthorized directive as a permanent project lesson, where it survives long after the untrusted page is evicted from the context window (Greshake et al. 2023). In an isolated evaluation sandbox, test harnesses inject synthetic credentials, probe unauthorized namespace boundaries, and retrieve documents embedded with indirect prompt injections. The test suite evaluates two enforcement layers: whether the runtime intercepts and suppresses unauthorized reads and writes, and whether an adversarial payload survives in downstream consolidated summaries after the primary source document has been withdrawn.

Greshake, Kai, Sahar Abdelnabi, Shailesh Mishra, Christoph Endres, Thorsten Holz, and Mario Fritz. 2023. “Not What You’ve Signed up for: Compromising Real-World LLM-Integrated Applications with Indirect Prompt Injection.” Proceedings of the 16th ACM Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security, 79–90. https://doi.org/10.1145/3605764.3623985.

Evaluators must capture both the model’s proposed actions and the runtime’s actual execution disposition. Logging both stages ensures that the evaluation distinguishes an attempted security breach from an executed vulnerability. The memory policy is accountable for the records it admits, indexes, and stages; the tool and runtime authorization subsystems are accountable for governing what the agent is permitted to execute against the external environment.

↪ The general adversarial-evaluation method developed in Adversarial Robustness and Red-Teaming specifies the trial and the attacker’s capabilities and measures attack success beside useful completion.

Checkpoint 6.4: Evidence across sessions

A proposed memory policy raises retrieval scores on a static snapshot. Its authors have not tested updates, deletion, or later task outcomes.

6.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Persistent memory systems fail when they grant a stored representation more operational authority than its underlying provenance supports, or when they treat write-time admission as a permanent guarantee of validity. Each fallacy or pitfall that follows is a design shortcut that produces silent state corruption, latency spikes, or decisions made on stale evidence, and most carry a cost that one of the chapter’s notebooks quantifies.

Fallacy: A near-duplicate memory carries no new information.

The 10:00-to-11:00 revision at the admission gate (section 6.8.2) is one instance of a wider class. A flipped flag (“notify me of failures” becoming “do not notify me of failures”) and a scoped exception (“no meetings before 11:00 on Fridays”) also land beside the record they modify in embedding space. Yet the first reverses it and the second must coexist with it. A similarity threshold fails differently on each, dropping the reversal as a repeat and collapsing the exception into the general rule, so the agent either keeps the prohibited behavior or loses the narrower constraint. High geometric proximity does not imply semantic equivalence. An admission pipeline must first establish entity identity, scope, record kind, and explicit update semantics. Geometric similarity should deduplicate storage only after verifying that the new record asserts no state change and no change of scope.

Pitfall: Treating a high-ranked record as current.

Vector similarity scores indicate semantic or topical affinity with a query, but they convey nothing about temporal freshness, transactional validity, or operational authority. In the trace that opens section 6.9, the outdated r17 excerpt of src/pool.py ranks first for the notification query. At turn 13 the agent consumed it without a version label and concluded that its own completed edit had been lost. It spent the following turns re-issuing that edit and then making speculative changes that pushed the repository further from a reviewed state. Coordinated generation checks prevent this particular cascade. Filtering 50 candidates adds 1.5 μs of local metadata lookup work under the stipulated scenario, excluding the durable coordination and fallback costs (notebook 6.4). While index rank identifies candidate relevance, the runtime must rely on provenance, version tags, visibility scope, and completion status to govern how a retrieved record is applied.

Fallacy: Staging more retrieved records gives the model more evidence.

Recall@\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) cannot decrease as \(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) grows, but task success does not follow it (Liu et al. 2024). In notebook 6.1, a staging policy that counts only raw document bodies admits 12 records that serialize to 7,040 tokens, overrunning a 6,144-token allowance before any of it reaches the model. Even within the allowance, the dilution and position effects that make staging depth a measured quantity rather than a maximum still apply. Staging depth must be calibrated against downstream task completion and execution budgets rather than optimizing recall in isolation (section 6.10).

Liu, Nelson F., Kevin Lin, John Hewitt, Ashwin Paranjape, Michele Bevilacqua, Fabio Petroni, and Percy Liang. 2024. “Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts.” Transactions of the Association for Computational Linguistics 12: 157–73. https://doi.org/10.1162/tacl_a_00638.

Pitfall: Re-indexing synchronously on every write to keep the index current.

Recomputing dense embeddings and rebuilding index graphs synchronously on every mutation introduces severe write latency and throughput bottlenecks. Under the stipulated costs of notebook 6.4, synchronous re-indexing adds between 88 ms and 1.26 s of blocking latency to every edit. Decoupling exclusion from rebuilding moves that work off the write path. The local exclusion updates take 0.4 μs, and each later query adds 1.5 μs of metadata lookups, excluding the common durable commit and coordination costs and any fallback retrieval. Replacement entries are built in the background. Blocking for fresh indexed representations can still be useful when dependent queries arrive inside the rebuild window and direct fallback cannot answer them within budget.

Fallacy: Every dense store needs an approximate index, because an exact scan is too slow.

Flat, exact vector scans scale linearly with the number of candidate vectors, but memory bandwidth alone carries substantial collections within tight latency budgets. In notebook 6.2, an unindexed scan across 10,000,000 vectors has a streaming floor of 307.2 ms. Applying a tenant or workspace filter beforehand reduces the candidate set to 10,000 vectors, dropping the streaming floor to 0.31 ms. A 10 ms query budget gives an optimistic ceiling of ~326,000 vectors before query encoding, top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) selection, and record fetches are charged. Exact scans deliver perfect recall under the metric with zero graph construction overhead, no index fragmentation, and immediate write availability. The bandwidth floor rules out infeasible scans. Measured full-request latency determines whether an exact scan meets the budget and whether an approximate index merits its maintenance overhead.

Pitfall: Evaluating memory on a static snapshot.

Benchmarking a memory subsystem against a static snapshot conceals the operational costs and failure modes that emerge under continuous mutation. Snapshot benchmarks ignore index drift, synchronization lag, compaction overhead, and stale-read anomalies. In notebook 6.5, modifying 15 files leaves a dense vector store without current entries for 612 ms, compared to 34.1 ms for a lexical and structural store. Every query in that window must fall back to a direct read or a partial result. Static tests also miss race conditions such as a delayed worker republishing superseded state or an explicitly deleted user preference persisting inside a rolled-up summary. Evaluate memory architectures using dynamic lifecycle fixtures (section 6.10), measuring end-to-end task completion, synchronization latency, and storage amplification across realistic write-read-delete workloads.

6.12 Summary

A software repair agent needs far more than an index that matches text embeddings against a bug report. Reliable long-term memory requires the system to determine what each retained record represents, which authoritative source supports it, how context budgets constrain its entry into later requests, and how downstream state changes when that underlying support is modified. Retrieval methods locate candidates under an explicit operational contract. The write lifecycle governs which records survive to become candidates. Comprehensive evaluation ensures that engineers do not mistake improved search recall for genuine task performance. Table 11 collects the quantitative relations governing long-term memory systems, linking each computational bound and operational threshold to the systems decision it informs.

Key Takeaways: Persistent information needs a lifecycle
  • Records retain different kinds of evidence: Primary source records, agent observations, synthesized lessons, and retrieval indexes serve different roles in an evidence chain. Explicit provenance and scope allow downstream systems to distinguish an empirical execution outcome from a speculative hypothesis. System deployers remain strictly accountable when an aggregated summary or derived heuristic contradicts its underlying source of record.
  • A retrieval result has a contract: Scope boundaries, access controls, version validity, payload budgets, and completion status dictate what enters prompt context. Formatting envelope overhead and metadata fields consume token allowances alongside record bodies, directly capping candidate capacity. An empty response from a partial or approximate search does not prove absence. Only a completed exhaustive search supports that conclusion within its declared scope and snapshot.
  • Search strategy follows the information need: Key-value lookups, lexical ranking, dense vector similarity, and structural graph traversal address distinct retrieval requirements. An exact scan supplies the recall baseline under the chosen metric. Its bandwidth floor (\(\EligibleVectorCount \EmbeddingDimension\ElementStorageSize/\Bandwidth\)) rules out infeasible scans, while measured full-request latency determines whether it meets the search budget. Every auxiliary vector index or inverted list introduces ongoing write amplification and synchronization overhead, which pays off only when it delivers measurable gains in task accuracy.
  • Relationships need evidence and a budget: Explicit graph edges connect scattered records into cohesive multi-hop evidence chains. Because candidate neighborhoods expand exponentially with traversal depth (\((\text{children}^{\text{depth}+1}-1)/(\text{children}-1)\)), retrieval pipelines must enforce strict depth and branching limits before scoring nodes. Traversal paths indicate topological reachability rather than verified runtime execution traces, so the agent must treat discovered paths as structural hypotheses that require independent corroboration.
  • Memory must change as its support changes: Updates, supersession, time-to-live expiration, and deletion policies dictate whether a record remains eligible for future prompts. Modifying or evicting a primary record invalidates all downstream summaries, embeddings, and inverted postings. Records that describe a user carry the consent and retention period under which they were admitted, and withdrawn consent or an elapsed retention period starts the same deletion procedure as an explicit request. Apparent near-duplicates frequently represent critical real-world state mutations rather than redundant text. Moreover, frequent read access indicates frequent use, but read volume alone does not validate the ongoing factual accuracy of stored state.
  • Exclusion and rebuilding incur different work: In the stipulated scenario, local exclusion lookups take microseconds while replacement indexing takes milliseconds to seconds. Durable coordination and fallback costs remain separate. Decoupling invalidation from background re-indexing through version-aware visibility windows (\(\MemoryEntryBirthGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry) \le \QueryWorkspaceGeneration < \MemoryEntryDeathGeneration(\DerivedMemoryEntry)\)) shields readers from stale state while asynchronous maintenance pipelines converge.
  • Evaluate memory policies across multi-session lifecycles: Offline ranking benchmarks reveal candidate retrieval precision, but multi-session evaluations expose how agents compound diagnostic errors or accumulate stale context over time.
