Assess competing memory architectures by their end-to-end task completion rate (\(\AcceptanceProbability\)), lifecycle contract compliance, and amortized cost per accepted task (\(\CostPerAcceptedTask\)), using isolated search metrics primarily to diagnose local failure modes.

Authority always resides with the source of record (principle 6). Retrieval therefore operates as an enforceable contract governing scope, freshness, and context allowances, rather than an unconstrained similarity lookup, and locating a candidate record does not grant permission to inject it uncritically into downstream execution. The write lifecycle extends this discipline to all derived artifacts that agents generate across sessions, including summaries, graph entities, vector embeddings, and cached lessons. Every transformation must preserve the explicit provenance, boundary conditions, and uncertainty of the original evidence rather than silently amplifying tentative diagnostic guesses into durable facts.

6.13 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Long before digital computers possessed the capacity to store vast text collections, researchers recognized that locating the right record among many is a selection problem rather than a storage problem. In his influential vision of the memex, Bush (1945) imagined a desk-sized device that would house an individual’s books, records, and correspondence, allowing the user to establish associative trails between related items. Bush observed that human memory operates by association rather than formal indexing, and he located the primary engineering difficulty in selection rather than physical storage capacity. Over the subsequent half-century, classical information retrieval transformed selection into mathematical measurement. Salton et al. (1975) formalized documents and queries as term-weight vectors ranked by geometric similarity in a shared vector space. Spärck Jones (1972) supplied the statistical foundation of inverse document frequency, demonstrating that rare terms provide far higher discriminative power than common ones. Building on probabilistic relevance frameworks, the approximations formulated by Robertson and Walker (1994) culminated in BM25, which remains a durable lexical ranking baseline across modern search engines. To make these rankings practical over large collections within tight latency budgets, search engines turned to inverted files, postings lists optimized for compact compression and fast intersection (Zobel and Moffat 2006). Lexical matching, however, inevitably faced the vocabulary mismatch problem: users and authors routinely describe identical concepts using different words (Furnas et al. 1987), an obstacle that ultimately required learned semantic representations.

Furnas, George W., Louis M. Gomez, Thomas K. Landauer, and Susan T. Dumais. 1987. “The Vocabulary Problem in Human-System Communication.” Communications of the ACM 30 (11): 964–71.
Blakeley, José A., Per-Åke Larson, and Frank Wm. Tompa. 1986. “Efficiently Updating Materialized Views.” Proceedings of the 1986 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 61–71. https://doi.org/10.1145/16894.16861.
Gupta, Ashish, and Inderpal Singh Mumick. 1995. “Maintenance of Materialized Views: Problems, Techniques, and Applications.” IEEE Data Engineering Bulletin 18 (2): 3–18.
Kung, H. T., and John T. Robinson. 1981. “On Optimistic Methods for Concurrency Control.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 6 (2): 213–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/319566.319567.
O’Neil, Patrick, Edward Cheng, Dieter Gawlick, and Elizabeth O’Neil. 1996. “The Log-Structured Merge-Tree (LSM-Tree).” Acta Informatica 33 (4): 351–85.

A second lineage came from databases and distributed systems, which tackled the maintenance half of the problem long before retrieval systems confronted mutable state. Because an agent’s retrieval index operates as a derived view over evolving execution logs and external environments, it inherits classical database maintenance obligations. Incremental view maintenance established the formal methods required to keep a derived table consistent with its mutating base relations without recomputing the entire view from scratch (Blakeley et al. 1986; Gupta and Mumick 1995). To coordinate concurrent writes without restrictive locking, optimistic concurrency control conditioned updates on validation checks against read sets (Kung and Robinson 1981). In distributed systems with weakly consistent replicas, epidemic replication algorithms proved that deletions must leave explicit tombstone markers. Without them, lagging peers interpret the absence of a record as missing data and resurrect the deleted item (Demers et al. 1987). Similarly, session guarantees formalized the exact consistency expectations, such as read-your-writes and monotonic reads, that clients require when querying lagging replicas (Terry et al. 1994). At the storage layer, the log-structured merge-tree turned append-only logging and deferred compaction of superseded entries into an essential systems pattern (O’Neil et al. 1996). Each of these mechanisms reappears in agent memory architectures, because a retrieval index is a ranked materialized view over mutable history as well as a search structure.

Learned retrieval joined these two lineages. Geometric relaxations such as locality-sensitive hashing initially emerged to help approximate nearest-neighbor search escape the curse of dimensionality (Indyk and Motwani 1998). Subsequently, product quantization (Jégou et al. 2011) and navigable proximity graphs (Malkov and Yashunin 2018) made sub-linear vector search practical at the scale of billions of embeddings (Johnson et al. 2019). In parallel, deep learning architectures began incorporating explicit memory components. Memory networks gave neural models a dedicated, addressable store to read and write outside their parameter weights (Weston et al. 2015). Open-domain question answering then paired learned neural retrievers with reading comprehension models (Chen et al. 2017), and dense passage retrieval (Karpukhin et al. 2020) alongside retrieval-augmented generation (Lewis et al. 2020) made the retriever an end-to-end learned component of text generation. Agent architectures extended this design by transforming the memory store into an active workspace that models write as well as read. Systems introduced interleaved search and reasoning traces (Yao et al. 2023), operating-system-inspired virtual context paging (Packer et al. 2023), verbalized self-reflections to correct past failures (Shinn et al. 2023), and continuous observation streams consolidated periodically into higher-level reflections (Park et al. 2023). From these combined lineages, agent systems inherit the engineering obligation this chapter develops. An agent’s memory record is dependable only to the extent that its provenance, version validity, and operational scope are explicitly guaranteed.

Johnson, Jeff, Matthijs Douze, and Hervé Jégou. 2019. “Billion-Scale Similarity Search with GPUs.” IEEE Transactions on Big Data 7 (3): 535–47. https://doi.org/10.1109/tbdata.2019.2921572.
Weston, Jason, Sumit Chopra, and Antoine Bordes. 2015. “Memory Networks.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Chen, Danqi, Adam Fisch, Jason Weston, and Antoine Bordes. 2017. “Reading Wikipedia to Answer Open-Domain Questions.” Proceedings of the 55th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 1870–79. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/P17-1171.
Karpukhin, Vladimir, Barlas Oguz, Sewon Min, Patrick Lewis, Ledell Wu, Sergey Edunov, Danqi Chen, and Wen-tau Yih. 2020. “Dense Passage Retrieval for Open-Domain Question Answering.” Proceedings of the 2020 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing (EMNLP), 6769–81.
Lewis, Patrick, Ethan Perez, Aleksandra Piktus, Fabio Petroni, Vladimir Karpukhin, Naman Goyal, Heinrich Küttler, et al. 2020. “Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 9459–74.
Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Packer, Charles, Vivian Fang, Shishir G. Patil, Kevin Lin, Sarah Wooders, and Joseph E. Gonzalez. 2023. “MemGPT: Towards LLMs as Operating Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.08560, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.08560.
Shinn, Noah, Federico Cassano, Ashwin Gopinath, Karthik Narasimhan, and Shunyu Yao. 2023. “Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 8634–52.

The following foundational works repay deeper study, organized in rough chronological progression:

  • Bush (1945) frames personal knowledge management around associative trails and selective retrieval, demonstrating that the central challenge of agent memory, extracting relevant context from an expansive repository, predates digital computing.
  • Robertson and Zaragoza (2009) provides the authors’ retrospective on the probabilistic relevance framework behind BM25, with a clear explanation of why term saturation and document length normalization take their specific mathematical forms.
  • Zobel and Moffat (2006) surveys inverted-file construction, postings compression, and fast top-\(\RetrievalCutoffCount\) query evaluation, detailing the systems engineering that underlies every production lexical index.
  • Terry et al. (1994) formalizes four client-centric session guarantees in a few compact pages. Read alongside section 6.9, it clarifies precisely which guarantees a retrieval gateway must enforce when managing asynchronous memory updates.
  • Jégou et al. (2011) and Malkov and Yashunin (2018) establish the two dominant architectural patterns in modern vector indexing. The former details product quantization for aggressive vector compression and fast asymmetric distance computation, while the latter introduces Hierarchical Navigable Small World (HNSW) graphs for sub-linear proximity routing. Both provide rigorous analyses of what each approximation sacrifices in recall vs. query latency.
  • Park et al. (2023) presents an end-to-end memory architecture for autonomous agents, combining continuous observation streams, multi-factor scoring (recency, importance, and relevance), and periodic hierarchical reflection. It serves as a concrete reference against which the record types and lifecycle contracts of this chapter can be evaluated.
Bush, Vannevar. 1945. “As We May Think.” The Atlantic Monthly 176 (1).
Robertson, Stephen, and Hugo Zaragoza. 2009. “The Probabilistic Relevance Framework: BM25 and Beyond.” Foundations and Trends in Information Retrieval 3 (4): 333–412. https://doi.org/10.1561/1500000019.
Zobel, Justin, and Alistair Moffat. 2006. “Inverted Files for Text Search Engines.” ACM Computing Surveys 38 (2): 6. https://doi.org/10.1145/1132956.1132959.
Jégou, Hervé, Matthijs Douze, and Cordelia Schmid. 2011. “Product Quantization for Nearest Neighbor Search.” IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence 33 (1): 117–28. https://doi.org/10.1109/TPAMI.2010.57.
Malkov, Yu A, and Dmitry A Yashunin. 2018. “Efficient and Robust Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search Using Hierarchical Navigable Small World Graphs.” IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence 42 (4): 824–36.
Park, Joon Sung, Joseph O’Brien, Carrie Jun Cai, Meredith Ringel Morris, Percy Liang, and Michael S. Bernstein. 2023. “Generative Agents: Interactive Simulacra of Human Behavior.” Proceedings of the 36th Annual ACM Symposium on User Interface Software and Technology, 1–22. https://doi.org/10.1145/3586183.3606763.

6.14 Exercises

Every quantity below is a hypothetical given chosen for round arithmetic, not a measurement of any system. Each problem ends in a decision; state the decision and the number that justifies it.

1. Serializing a smaller call (Basic). A call permits 16,384 tokens. The runtime reserves 1,024 for output, and all other serialized input occupies 11,264. The result envelope costs 96 tokens, and each retrieved record carries a 300-token body and 60 tokens of source, version, and provenance metadata.

  1. How many complete records fit, and how many tokens remain?
  2. A retrieval limit that counts only bodies would admit how many records, and by how much would their serialization overshoot the allowance?
  3. The task owner wants 13 records staged. Metadata cannot be dropped, because the version labels are what let the model tell r17 from r18. What is the largest body excerpt per record that meets the request?
  4. Two more tool results adding 512 tokens of other input arrive before the call. How many complete records fit now, and which reservation should the runtime recompute before dispatch?

2. Where the exact scan stops paying (Basic). A tenant’s store holds \(\EligibleVectorCount = 2{,}000{,}000\) vectors of dimension \(\EmbeddingDimension = 1{,}024\) in FP16 (\(\ElementStorageSize = 2\) bytes). The host sustains 100 GB/s of memory bandwidth and 2 TFLOP/s of peak arithmetic, and each search has a 5 ms latency budget.

  1. Compute the bytes, FLOPs, and arithmetic intensity of one exact scan, and compare the intensity with the host’s ridge point.
  2. What is the bandwidth floor for one full scan, and what optimistic eligible-vector ceiling does that floor set under the budget?
  3. A scope filter restricts most queries to one project of 150,000 vectors. Does the bandwidth floor rule out an exact scan, and what measurement would decide whether it meets the search budget?
  4. Would storing the full corpus as INT8 bring the bandwidth floor of an unfiltered exact scan within budget?

3. How much agreement buys (Basic). Lexical search returns P, Q, R, S in that order, and dense search returns S, T, P, U. Every candidate is fully scoped to the same r18 snapshot, ties break by candidate identifier, and the runtime stages three records.

  1. With \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant = 60\), compute every fused score with equation 3 and the staged set.
  2. Find the largest rank \(\FusionInputRank\) at which a candidate appearing at rank \(\FusionInputRank\) in both lists still outscores a candidate at rank 1 in only one list, for \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant = 60\) and for \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant = 1\).
  3. Candidate Q is the reproducer test the agent must read. Is there any positive \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant\) at which Q outranks P? How does Q reach the staged set at \(\RankFusionSmoothingConstant = 60\), and what should the runtime do instead of relying on that?

4. How much the task names (Intermediate). Transfer the choice of retrieval policy (section 6.4) to the research agent, whose store holds notes distilled from fetched pages. Every model round takes 4 s, and retrieval operations are fast enough to ignore. Each note serializes to 600 tokens with its metadata, the retrieval allowance is 6,144 tokens, and everything retrieved stays in context until the answer. Three policies are available. A direct read stages exactly the records the task names, with no extra round. Pre-staging stages the top 8 ranked notes before the first call; when that set lacks needed evidence, the model falls back to probing for what is missing. A model-directed probe costs one model round and returns 2 notes. Every policy ends with one answer round. Three tasks arrive:

  • R1: “Reconcile pinned notes n-4412 and n-4413.” The evidence is those two notes. Pre-staging captures both with probability 0.9, and a miss costs one probe round. Directed search needs one probe round.
  • R2: “What has earlier work in this project established about battery fade under fast charging?” The evidence is three unnamed notes. Pre-staging captures all three with probability 0.8, and a miss costs one probe round. Directed search needs two probe rounds.
  • R3: “Trace where the 40 percent figure in n-4412 came from.” The evidence is n-4412 and three further notes, each found only from a citation in the one before. Pre-staging includes n-4412 but captures the whole chain with probability 0.1, and a miss costs three dependent probe rounds. Directed search needs four dependent probe rounds.
  1. For R1, compute the expected latency and staged tokens of each policy. Which policy wins, and what does pre-staging add that the research agent’s binding constraint makes costly?
  2. For R2, compare pre-staging with directed search in expected latency, expected staged tokens, and worst-case staged tokens against the allowance. Which should the runtime choose?
  3. For R3, compute the same quantities for pre-staging and for directed search. Why is pre-staging’s lower expected latency not a reason to choose it?
  4. Design a policy for R3 that combines two of the three, and compute its latency and staged tokens. State the selection rule the three tasks illustrate, and name the property of a returned record that stays the same whichever policy retrieves it.

5. Fitting an index in memory (Intermediate). A research store holds \(\EligibleVectorCount = 50{,}000{,}000\) passage vectors of dimension \(\EmbeddingDimension = 768\) in FP16 on a host with 64 GB of memory available for the index. The candidate designs are an exact scan of the raw vectors, HNSW with 32 eight-byte links per vector, and IVF-PQ with \(\PqSubvectorCount = 96\) one-byte subcodes, \(\CoarseCellCount = 10{,}000\) FP16 coarse centroids, and an 8-byte record identifier per stored code.

  1. Which designs fit the memory budget? Compute the resident bytes of each.
  2. For IVF-PQ, give the subvector dimension, the per-query lookup-table size with FP32 entries, and the compression ratio over the FP16 vectors.
  3. With \(\ProbedCellCount = 16\) and evenly filled cells, how many bytes of codes does one query scan, and how does that compare with the bytes read to compare the query against every coarse centroid? What does the comparison suggest about the next optimization?
  4. Full-precision re-scoring of a 100-candidate shortlist reads vectors from secondary storage. How many bytes does that fetch, what distance accuracy does it recover, and which upstream misses can it not repair?

6. Synchronous re-indexing in a repair session (Intermediate). Each edit in a repair session changes 5 files and 15 indexed chunks. Stipulated costs are 4 ms to parse a file, 2 ms to update one chunk’s postings, 40 ms per local embedding batch of up to 8 chunks, 150 ms per batch on a remote service, 50 ns for a local in-memory exclusion update per file, and 30 ns for a local lookup on one of 40 candidates per query. All strategies share a durable source-commit and coordination boundary whose costs are excluded, along with fallback retrieval costs. Background rebuilding starts immediately after that commit and executes the stipulated stages serially without queueing. A session performs 60 edits, and the model’s next dependent query arrives 2 s after each source commit.

  1. What write-path time does synchronous re-indexing add per edit with the local service, with the remote service, and over the whole session?
  2. What local metadata work does exclusion add per write and per query, and which costs are absent from this comparison?
  3. Under background re-indexing with exclusion, is any dependent query served while replacement entries are missing?
  4. A new runtime issues an automatic verification search 50 ms after every source commit. Which queries now fall inside the rebuild interval, what does the retrieval layer return for them, and does that change justify synchronous re-indexing?

7. Deletion deadlines in a support store (Intermediate). Transfer the invalidation analysis to the support agent. Its store holds 80,000,000 memory vectors under an HNSW graph, kept at constant size because inserts balance removals. Each day 2,000 customers request full deletion of their 40 records apiece, and 20,000 customers supersede one preference, tombstoning its old vector. Compaction is triggered at \(\TombstonedRecordFraction = 0.10\), rebuilds the live graph at a stipulated 20,000 vectors per second, and each tombstone occupies 16 bytes of an in-memory exclusion set. Retention policy requires deleted content to be physically removed within 30 days.

  1. How many days until the \(\TombstonedRecordFraction\) trigger fires, and what is \(\TombstonedRecordFraction\) when the retention deadline arrives? Which constraint sets the compaction schedule?
  2. How long does one compaction take, and how large is the exclusion set just before it?
  3. Each superseded preference also appears in two session summaries of about 1,000 tokens each. How many summary tokens per day would regeneration cost, and what cheaper treatment does the write lifecycle allow for a summary that is only historical?
  4. The repair agent’s binding hazard is its own edits making indexed copies stale. Name the support agent’s binding hazard and the mechanism in this chapter that answers it.

8. Typed edges as a budget control (Intermediate). A structural graph around a seed function has, on average, 4 outgoing call edges and 10 import edges per node. The retrieval allowance is 8,192 tokens, each node record serializes to 200 tokens, and edges plus the envelope take 150 tokens. Treat the neighborhood as an exact tree with no repeated nodes.

  1. Through what depth does a traversal restricted to call edges fit, and through what depth does one that also follows imports fit?
  2. What is the largest number of complete nodes the allowance holds?
  3. Could trimming node records to 120 tokens let a depth-3 call-only traversal fit? What is the largest node record that would?
  4. Why would the exact-tree count overstate the staged size of a real repository graph, and why is that no reason to drop the depth bound?

9. Does a dense index pay for itself? (Advanced). Two memory policies serve the same repair workload with one attempt per task. Policy L, lexical plus structural retrieval, accepts 55 percent of tasks at $0.80 per attempt. Policy D adds dense retrieval, accepts 62 percent, and stages more evidence, raising the attempt cost to $0.84. It also re-embeds 20 chunks for each of 6 edits per task at $0.0005 per chunk and carries $0.01 per task of amortized vector storage. Its encoder processes 50 chunks per second.

  1. Compute \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) for both policies with equation 1.2. Which is cheaper per accepted task?
  2. What acceptance rate must Policy D reach to break even with Policy L?
  3. How long does each edit leave the dense index without current entries, and what must the retrieval layer do for queries in that window?
  4. The vendor of Policy D’s encoder reports that Recall@10 rose from 0.60 to 0.80 on a static snapshot. Critique the claim as evidence for adopting Policy D, and design the trial that would settle the decision.

10. Redesigning a freshness scheme (Advanced). A team proposes to keep a repair index current with a per-file dirty flag that is set when an edit commits and cleared when a rebuild finishes, plus a five-minute age limit measured from the worker’s source capture. At expiry, an entry becomes ineligible immediately and is scheduled for re-indexing; until a replacement appears, the gateway returns an incomplete result. Every replica applies each primary update exactly 2 s after it commits there, and a publication replaces the file’s current entries. Consider one timeline. At \(\TimeInstant = -0.1\) s worker W1 captures src/pool.py at r17 and begins indexing it. At \(\TimeInstant = 0\) the agent commits r18 and the flag is set. At \(\TimeInstant = 0.5\) s worker W2 publishes r18 entries and clears the flag. At \(\TimeInstant = 0.7\) s W1 finishes and publishes its r17 entries. No other publication occurs before W1’s entries expire. The agent queries the file every 3 s from \(\TimeInstant = 3\) s onward.

  1. Ignoring replicas, under the proposal, for how long are r17 entries served as current, and how many of the agent’s queries can receive them?
  2. Assign generation intervals to the r17 and r18 entries and apply equation 4 with \(\QueryWorkspaceGeneration\) = r18. Which entries are admitted, and what must W1’s publication check do?
  3. A query issued at \(\TimeInstant = 1\) s reaches a replica, carrying the log sequence number of the r18 commit. Using the read-your-writes rule, compare a 0.5 s wait bound with a 1.5 s bound in added latency and in the evidence returned.
  4. Write the redesigned protocol as at most five rules, and state which part of the proposal, if any, survives.

What’s Next: From memory policy to tool execution
The memory policies in this chapter already assume controlled operations that read sources, search indexes, update records, and return bounded observations. A retrieved record names the version it observed, but whether the source still holds that version when an edit commits, and whether an update that timed out took effect at all, are questions no stored record can answer. Settling them requires an interface that separates a generated request from its authorization, execution, and reported outcome, the boundary Part III, Tool Use, develops. Tool Calling begins with the tool-call contract through which the repair’s proposed reads and writes become permitted operations, so that retrieved information is never mistaken for permission to act.
